Global Data Watch
•
Inflation persistence, labor market rebound point to an end to Fed magical 
thinking 
•
CB divergences should emerge as global common inflation impulse fades 
•
A consumption downshift is underway but doesn’t appear threatening 
•
Next week: Soft Oct. US RS and China activity readings; Fed minutes  
Losing my religion
Our macroeconomic outlook has stood on two pillars this year. The first is that 
global growth would remain resilient as boomy tech spending is complemented by 
a cyclical rebound in non-tech business demand. Cyclical lift, fueled by broad-
based profit gains and supportive financial conditions, is expected to also boost 
hiring. The second pillar is that elevated core inflation largely reflects underlying 
forces promoting persistence. Thus, even under optimistic forecasts for lower 
energy prices and tech price increases, US and global core inflation is poised to 
remain sticky as long as solid demand growth maintains firm pricing power. 
The global economy has been on a bumpy ride this year, marked by a large energy 
sector shock that raised anxiety around growth resilience. Despite growth’s 
resilience through this shock, the labor market improvement has also proved 
disappointing. US dynamics around midyear with evidence of a pickup in US non-
tech spending were followed by signs of firmer labor demand. Outside the US the 
news is more mixed, but our global output and employment PMI have bounced 
(Figure 1). Notwithstanding an anticipated current quarter downshift in global 
consumer spending – reflected in an October global auto sales drop and next 
week’s expected US and China soft retail sales reports – confidence in our upbeat 
growth views has increased. Resilient demand has been accompanied by a rebound 
in global core inflation (ex China and Türkiye) to a 3.4%ar in the three months 
through October. Signs of continued goods price pressures point to inflation 
persistence, belying the hopes for a quick inflation decline. 
Taken together, recent developments raise the conviction that we are close to the 
end of a long period of magical thinking. Since the end of the  2022-23 tightening 
cycle, markets and central banks have maintained a view that solid growth and 
earnings can be sustained, while inflation moves lower, allowing central banks to 
maintain supportive stances. Amid expected solid growth, the response by 
financial market conditions to an expected shift away from magical thinking by 
central banks is a central issue for next year’s outlook.

Global Data Watch
13 November 2026
J P M O R G A N
This week’s Fed communications underscore that a policy 
pivot has begun away from its New Testament stance of for-
giving our inflation sins while promoting the dogma of 
immaculate disinflation. This view was maintained in the 
September SEP – which projected inflation moving back to 
target amid sustained above-potential growth. It continued 
through their recent October pause, which reiterated guidance 
for a shallow and gradual policy adjustment.  
Incoming data are challenging the Fed’s beliefs on multiple 
fronts. Even before this week’s CPI surprise, Governor Waller 
highlighted concerns about the breadth of recent core price 
rises (Table 1). Continued build-up of upstream pressure is an 
additional concern as the latest three-month annualized rates 
of increase of core PPI and consumer import prices are 
5.5%saar and 3.3%ar, respectively. 
The forward-looking implications of firming labor demand 
are also influencing the Fed. Employment looks to be align-
ing with our forecast for gains of 100k or faster per month 
amid an underlying environment of weak labor supply. The 
October report posted a stable unemployment rate, showing 
that workers can be drawn into the labor market to meet ris-
ing demand. But Fed officials recognize that labor markets 
are tight and that wage inflation will likely firm in an envi-
ronment of sustained solid demand. 
It is premature to anticipate that the FOMC will communicate 
a need to apply the brakes on the expansion any time soon. In 
the previous hiking cycle, it was only at the July 2022 press 
conference that Chair Powell’s press conference inserted into 
prepared remarks the statement that “reducing inflation is 
likely to require a period of below-trend growth and some 
softening of labor market conditions.” It was removed in 
December 2023. However, the upcoming December meeting 
looks likely to deliver a hike, with a median voter anticipating 
multiple rate hikes next year. This would guide the Fed to go 
beyond removing last year’s easing and signal an intent to 
return to a policy stance intended to restrain demand.

Diversity vs. the commons 
The guidance from a balanced Taylor rule aligns with the 
view that the FOMC should move beyond the 4.5% policy 
rate in place before its 2025 easing (Table 2). But outside 
Japan, domestic fundamentals do not point toward further 
hikes. A broad round of DM hikes is anticipated next month, 
highlighting the importance of this year’s powerful global 
common inflation impulse in promoting synchronized hikes. 
With energy prices now moving lower and other DM coun-
tries unlikely to see material labor market pressures, a diver-
gence between the Fed and other central banks could emerge 
next year. 
A key risk to reasserting central bank diversity is the potential 
lasting impact of this year’s inflation shock. A recent study 
from the Federal Reserve Board points out an increase in both 
DM inflation persistence and the pass-through of energy price 
shocks during this decade (Figure 2). Our own forecast down-
plays the magnitude of the energy price passthrough, which 
relied heavily on the 2021-2022 experience. But we do 
believe that a broader set of cost pressures have pushed infla-
tion higher this year and recognize longer tails as a result of 
increased persistence in the inflation process.

Western Europe cross currents
A disappointing 3Q26 stall in Euro area employment is a 
reminder that the region’s expansion has not broadened to the 
same degree as the US. Moreover, the drag from natural gas 
prices remains significant and is contributing to rate hike 
expectations hike. Political risks around upcoming elections 
and recent protests pose risks, but protests appear to be losing 
momentum, Overall, growth is still tracking above 1% and 
there are cross-current and should remain there if we are right 
that energy price pressures abate as we turn into the new year. 
 
By contrast, UK GDP grew at a strong 3%ar last quarter sup-
ported by resilient consumption and demand for business ser-
vices. Although we expect growth to slow this quarter, Sep-

tember’s gain prompted us to raise our 4Q forecast by 
0.5%-pt to 1.5%. With natural gas prices rebounding again 
and the labor market recovering, pressure is building for the 
BoE to move faster. We expect a February hike, but Decem-
ber is now a close call ahead of next week’s inflation and 
labor-market data.
BoJ to make haste slowly
The BoJ’s October summary of opinions reinforced its inten-
tion to continue gradually raising rates toward neutral, consis-
tent with our expectation of a quarterly hiking pace through 
mid 2027. This week’s firm business sentiment and resilient 
services consumption suggest the economy can absorb further 
tightening, but aggregate resilience masks widening dispari-
ties. Accommodative financial conditions continue to benefit 
large companies and higher-income households, while persis-
tent inflation squeezes smaller firms and lower- and middle-
income households. Modest rate hikes could therefore be 
absorbed in firms’ margins and help rebalance growth without 
materially weakening overall activity.
China: Struggling to find balance 
AI-related external demand and a rebound in energy imports 
boosted China’s trade flow through October. But inflation 
data suggest that upstream cost pressures are still not translat-
ing into domestic reflation. Next week’s activity data should 
show IP flat on the month (though still strong annual gains), 
retail sales down 0.1% m/m and a marginally slower pace of 
fixed investment contraction. Our forecast for 5%ar GDP 
growth this quarter and next still hinges on the delivery of fis-
cal easing. A possible tax on government-bond interest 
income risks raising borrowing costs before domestic demand 
has strengthened, places focus on the sequencing of policy 
action.
Oil relief, Fed pressure, and El Niño
The recent fall in oil prices provides a broadly dovish impulse 
for EM central banks, but higher DM rates, renewed food-
price pressures, and weaker currencies limit the relief. In 
EMEA, the move lower in oil is likely to dominate in the near 
term, particularly in CEE, reinforcing our calls for unchanged 
rates in Czechia and Poland, modest easing in Hungary, and 
continued 100bp cuts in Türkiye. Elsewhere, higher US rates 
and currency weakness are a greater constraint: lower oil 
reduces the urgency for a South African hike, but rand weak-
ness keeps the risk alive, while ILS depreciation leaves little 
room for further Israeli cuts.
In EM Asia, the oil relief has been offset by renewed food-
price pressures and strong domestic demand in India, with 
October CPI showing a worrying firming in core inflation. 
We now expect inflation to remain around the RBI’s 6% 
upper tolerance limit through mid 2027 and forecast 25bp 
hikes at the next three meetings, taking the terminal rate to 
6.25%, with a meaningful risk of a 50bp move in December. 
The BoT minutes also reinforced our out-of-consensus call 
for three hikes next year, with a hawkish dissent likely in 
December ahead of a first hike in March.
Romania: Turning the corner
Romania continues moving in a constructive direction, with 
better growth and planned fiscal consolidation. Growth sur-
prised positively this week, with 3Q26 GDP jumping 2.1%q/q 
sa, well above our 1% forecast, reflecting strong investment 
and a better harvest despite the drought. The growth surge 
should prove transitory, but we still revised our 2026 GDP 
forecast to -0.2% from -0.5%, while keeping 2027 at 2.5%. 
Politically, tensions have eased following approval of the 
Niculescu government, while fiscal consolidation remains 
broadly on track. The new government appears committed to 
further consolidation toward a 5.0% budget deficit in 2027. 
Budget negotiations will become the main political focus over 
the next couple of months, as PSD will need PNL support to 
pass the budget. 
But despite a large negative output gap and the slowest wage 
growth in CEE, inflation remains elevated. Headline CPI rose 
to 6.9%oya in October, and we revised our end-2026 forecast 
to 7.3%, partly reflecting weaker FX. We expect disinflation 
to resume in 1Q27 and inflation to stabilize around 4% later 
in 2027, allowing NBR cuts from May. 
LatAm fiscal ambitions
Colombia and Brazil face a common fiscal credibility test, but 
with different levels of ambition. Colombia’s proposed 
2.35%-of-GDP fiscal adjustment, in 2027 reinforced by possi-
ble IMF support, could lower risk premia and eventually cre-
ate room for BanRep easing, although the effective tightening 
may be closer to 1.6–1.8%. Brazil continues to rely on piece-
meal measures that do little to strengthen the underlying fis-
cal position, leaving next year’s primary balance likely to fall 
materially short of the government’s 0.5%-of-GDP target.

Taiwan (China).
Global inflation rebounded from a midyear moderation, with 
headline prices on track to rise 5.5%ar over three months 
through October as cost pressures meet resilient demand. 
Core goods inflation is near 2.5%ar this quarter, with surveys 
signaling continued pressure. Global auto sales fell 2.3%m/m 
in October on broad-based weakness, while ex-China sales 
are tracking below our 3%-4%ar 2H26 contraction call. Amid 
central bank hikes, G-4 lending standards tightened only 
modestly. Business and household loan demand fell, but 
capex activity remains robust.
See here for real-time snapshots and methodology reports.
JPM forecast evolution, GDP and CPI
Our tracking of 3Q global growth ticked up to 3.2%ar this 
week. Our 4Q forecast was unchanged at 2.7%ar (Figure 1). 
Our forecast for 4Q core inflation edged up to 3.1%ar (Figure 
2).

Global Economic Research
13 November 2026
J P M O R G A N
We stay moderately positive on European credit for 2027, 
favoring up-in-quality trades as following the recent wid-
ening in spreads. Consequently, our top trades include OW 
1-3Y IG, OW BBs over Bs and OW high yield bonds versus 
leveraged loans. Euro IG spreads sit at 113bp, and we forecast 
our 2027 year spread target at 100bp, implying a 7.0% total 
return, with French sovereign risk and weaker technicals lim-
iting further compression. In HY, we view the widening to 
387bp as a reset rather than the start of a secular bear market, 
and set our 2027 year-end spread target to 350bp, implying a 
8.25% total return. We look for default rates of 4% in bonds 
and loans for FY27, followed by 3% and 4% respectively in 
FY28(European Credit Outlook & Strategy 2027, Nov 11th).
US IG for AI-linked supply continues to be led by asset 
managers, while insurers remain underweight. Insurers’ 
estimated AI-related assets are around 2.27% of their asset 
base, well below the 6.87% that AI issuers represent in the 
JULI index, suggesting capacity for additional exposure as 
wider spreads create more attractive entry points. Public hold-
ings data covering roughly 45% of hyperscaler par suggest 
insurers own around 25% of hyperscaler bonds versus 53% 
for asset managers, while data-center ownership is around 16-
17% and 79%, respectively. Looking ahead, we focus on 
GPU financings, where shorter tenors must compete with a 
much broader set of front-end fixed-income alternatives, 
requiring a wider relative-value hurdle.(Who is buying all the 
hyperscaler bonds?, Nov 10th).

Source: J.P. Morgan
Bonds
This week, DM rates sold off across the board, with the US 
curve leading the underperformance. The US curve bear flat-
tened, with the 2Y up +33bp over the week, following the 
firm US inflation print and hawkish Fed rhetoric, resulting in 
a repricing of ~88bp of cumulative Fed hikes by September 
2027 versus ~47bp priced over this same period just one 
week ago.
In the Euro area, we stay neutral duration in the near-term as 
the market uncertainty regarding the Fed, the AI landscape 
and US-Iran/energy developments continues to take center 
stage. We neutralize the OW Germany vs. US stance after the 
relative US underperformance and believe that intermediate 
German yields remain attractive for long term investors. In 
the UK, gilt yields moved higher over the week, on the back 
of higher gas prices and in sympathy with the rise in US 
Treasury yields. The market is pricing ~20bp for the Decem-
ber meeting and a cumulative of +90bp of hikes by the end of 
2027. We stay received Dec26 MPC OIS as there is uncera-
tinty on the knock-on effects from higher eneryg, but realzie 
that the a large move in gas prices or the CPI services print 
would allow the BoE to hike next month. In Japan, JGB 
yields moved higher across the curve, led by 10Y, amid ris-
ing U.S. Treasury yields and the BoJ’s “Summary of Opin-
ions” which reiterated its stance on continuing to raise policy 
rates. Stronger U.S. CPI and hawkish Fed commentary drove 
further U.S. rate repricing and yen weakness, which in turn 
strengthened expectations for additional BoJ tightening and 
extended the JGB selloff.
In EM, we stay MW rates overall, with OWs in Hungary, 
Colombia, Mexico, Paraguay vs. UW Peru and Chile rates.
Credit
We forecast another record year for US HG gross issuance 
in 2027, with gross supply of $2.1tr (+5% YoY). However, 
the net absorption burden looks more manageable: $1tr of 
maturities leaves net issuance at $1.06tr (+13%), while rising 
coupon income of $556bn (+12%) reduces “net net” supply to 
$508bn (+14%), modestly below the 2020 record. Additional-
ly, we estimate $22bn in calls and a meaningful step up in 
tenders in 2027, both of which are further sources of reinvest-
ment cash. Supply will be increasingly concentrated in Tech 
and Media, with $612bn of gross issuance (+23%), driven by 
hyperscalers, data centers and GPU financing. Regarding 
M&A, we forecast only $144bn in M&A supply, down 52% 
YoY after a record 2026. (2027 US HG Issuance Forecast, 
Nov 13th).

Global Economic Research
J.P. Morgan Market Watch
13 November 2026
J P M O R G A N
Currencies
In DM FX, markets face competing signals from falling ener-
gy prices and hawkish Fed repricing, but USD sensitivity to 
higher terminal rates is dominating. We stay bullish USD  and 
stay long vs. EUR, SEK and CAD. We re-initiate pro-cyclical 
exposure via NOK longs vs. EUR and SEK as oil sensitivity 
fades (FX Markets Weekly Update, Nov 13th).
In EM FX, we stay MW in the Model Portfolio holding OWs 
in CNY, HUF, TRY, COP, PYG while UW RSD. In outright 
trades (FX Markets Weekly, Nov 6th).
Commodities
Energy open interest (OI) collapsed 14% WoW to $871bn, 
its lowest since August, as the blue-wave mid-terms erod-
ed the geopolitical risk premium across crude oil and 
European natural gas. Crude led net contract-based out-
flows of -$27bn WoW, and we estimate the global oil market 
swung to a surplus of 1.1 mbd in September, widening to 1.4 
mbd in October on recovering Middle Eastern flows and 
weak demand. Precious metals OI rose 4% WoW to $271bn, 
with gold inflows ($2.9bn) which is encouraging for our 
medium-term bullish view after its post-election pop and 
safe-haven resilience. Copper pricing drove base-metals 
inflows ($4.2bn), caught between Chinese tightness and US 
AI and tariff questions. Agricultural OI fell to a four-month 
low of $386bn as energy weakness hit the soy complex (Com-
modity Market Positioning & Flows, Nov 10th).
 European natural gas prices stay elevated as cold, wind-
less, dry weather, HDDs at 20 MTD vs a 10-year average of 
16, accelerates withdrawals from historically low storage. For 
the week of November 2-8, NWE+UK demand averaged 800 
Mcm/day, up 30% WoW and 34% YoY, the highest for this 
time of year since 2022. With MTD wind and hydro output 
down 36% and 48% YoY, a 100 Mcm/day residential demand 
jump led by Germany, France and the UK pushed gas burns 
up 50% WoW to a seasonal high near 48 GW/day. Net with-
drawals hit 208 Mcm/day — steepest since at least 2020 — 
with an ~20-day earlier withdrawal start adding ~4pp to the 
storage deficit. Storage stands at 65.8% full (32.8 Bcm) vs 
79% last year. The November outlook has shifted from warm-
er-than-normal to above the 10-year HDD average; we expect 
TTF to hold near current levels until forecasts normalise, with 
balances only loosening if a warmer second half materialises  
(European Natural Gas: Demand & Storage Tracker, Nov 
10th).
LNG exports from the Middle East are closing the gap 
with oil as Qatar and the UAE ramp up shipments 
through the Strait of Hormuz. Hormuz flows are now 
around 50% of prewar levels for both oil and LNG, though 
total oil exports are largely back to prewar levels once bypass 
pipelines and ship-to-ship transfers are included. November-
to-date, eight Qatari vessels have exited the Strait, all in dark 
mode, a postwar record, lifting Qatari utilization to 60%, 
while combined Qatar and UAE utilization runs near 50% on 
a 14-day moving average. Observable loadings show just 10-
12%, as more activity shifts to dark mode and obscures real-
time tracking, so we monitor deliveries as the more reliable 
gauge. Rising regional supply has pressured TTF lower 
despite tightening fundamentals from low wind and colder 
weather, and we expect Qatar to keep ramping gradually 
absent escalation or a ceasefire (Global LNG Supply & Ship-
ping Tracker, Nov 9th).

would weigh heavily on the expansion, if not end it. The good 
news is that the latest data support our call for a rebound in 
business hiring. Actual employment data, including the solid 
October US payroll report, have begun to firm, while survey 
data point to further improvement ahead.
At the same time, the evidence for an AI tech boom in pro-
ductivity is far from clear. Estimates of AI’s potential macro-
economic effects vary, while evidence that those effects have 
already appeared in the aggregate data is limited. At the cau-
tious end, Daron Acemoglu estimates a TFP gain of less than 
0.5% cumulative over the next decade.1 At the optimistic end, 
Aghion and Bunel estimate that AI could add 0.8 to 
1.3%-point to annualized productivity growth.2 The empirical 
evidence so far is unconvincing: Danish data show no 
significant effect on earnings or hours; US executives report 
uneven gains and little net employment reduction; and 
aggregate US data continue to show AI’s clearest effects in 
investment and selected sectors rather than a broad 
transformation of output or labor demand.3

This note considers the evolution of technology shocks on the 
macroeconomy. Based on a careful decomposition, the analy-
sis below shows that almost all of the surge in labor produc-
tivity growth in the US in recent years owes to a combination 
of capital-deepening (investment) and increases in factor uti-
lization. Both of these can boost growth in measured labor

The narrative of robust AI-led growth without jobs is 
not sustainable
•
The boom in labor productivity adds fuel to this nar-
rative
•
However, a careful understanding of the evolution of 
technology shocks tells a different story
•
The current productivity boom owes more to capital 
deepening and factor utilization than technology gains 
•
This is not to say technology gains will not come, just 
that they will take longer than some are hoping
•
This supports our call for a cyclical rise in hiring, 
sticky inflation, and higher rates
The global economy has proven resilient despite the head-
winds from the US war on trade and Iran. Global GDP ex-
China has boomed 2.5% annualized since the start of 2025 
(Figure 1). Growth in consumer spending has been equally 
robust, a remarkable outcome given the near stall in real pur-
chasing power as job growth has slowed and inflation has 
surged. In past research, we have argued that the resilience of 
consumer spending owes to robust wealth gains and house-
holds' hope that the real labor income growth would rebound. 
Consequently, our outlook expects the source of growth to 
rotate from wealth effects to more sustainable labor income.

There is an alternative narrative, however, that the surge in 
labor productivity implied by strong GDP growth absent 
employment is a more fundamental shift owing to AI-related 
tech improvements. This narrative is less constructive than it 
may seem, however. Households will not keep spending with-
out jobs. The saving rate may be able to drop further, but it is 
unlikely to extend indefinitely. Eventually, households will 
reassess their income prospects in the face of ongoing disap-
pointment and begin to view the shortfall as a reduction in 
their permanent income. The resulting correction in spending

removed, estimates of total factor productivity (TFP) growth 
in the US have been softer than labor productivity (Figure 2). 
This exercise has measurement challenges, and theoretical 
arguments can be made about the role of the technology 
embedded in capital deepening. Still, the data suggest that the 
broad-based disembodied multifactor productivity tech boom 
that characterized the 1990s has yet to arrive for the AI boom. 
Few doubt that AI will be a major technological revolution. 
However, like most technological advances, the benefits 
could take longer to arrive than consensus anticipates. In the 
meantime, a more mundane but crucial cyclical lift in busi-
ness hiring will be the key to extending the expansion. The 
implications for inflation are clear. Combined with weak 
labor supply, tightening labor markets will temper disinfla-
tion. Moreover, the part of the rise in measured labor produc-
tivity owing to rising factor utilization will limit any cushion-
ing of unit labor costs. Altogether, these dynamics reinforce 
the “strong growth, sticky inflation, higher rates” narrative we 
have been telling since our global 2026 year-ahead outlook.  
The “I” and the “RoI” of the tech boom
Technological innovation comes in two stages. The first is the 
surge in investment as businesses see new opportunities. The 
release of the first generative AI models in 2024 opened the 
floodgates to new possibilities, sparking a surge in invest-
ments. Global capex has jumped 7%ar since the start of 2025 
and is now running at a double-digit pace this year. Tech 
capex in the US has skyrocketed 30% over the past year.

The first step in a technology boom is the investment stage 
(“I”). During this stage, the beneficiaries are not the business-
es doing the capex but, rather, the businesses producing the 
capital. This is clear in the US, where the bulk of global tech 
and Jeffrey S. Allen. 2026. “The AI Buildout and the Economy: 
Publicly Available Data to Assess AI’s Impact.” FEDS Notes. 
Washington: Federal Reserve Board, July 17, 2026.
capex is taking place. While capex growth has contributed 
nearly 1%-point annualized to US GDP growth over the past 
year, most of this has been imported (Figure 3). The net add 
to GDP is thus relatively modest. Instead, most of the value-
added of the roughly $1.2tn surge in hyperscaler tech capex 
(with trillions more planned) goes to the Asian manufactur-
ers. Of the 5.6% rise in global manufacturing since 2024, 
Asia has contributed 4.7%-points (Figure 4).

The next stage in a technology boom is the “return on invest-
ment” (“RoI”). This is when investments begin to pay off, but 
it takes time. In 1987, economist Robert Solow famously 
quipped, “You can see the computer age everywhere but in 
the productivity statistics.” The observation captured the dis-
connect between the rapid diffusion of a transformative com-
puting technology that had been invested in over the prior two 
decades and the surprisingly limited evidence of its benefits 
in the economic data. This, of course, changed dramatically 
with the explosion of structural total factor productivity 
growth in the 1990s (Figure 1 above).

Although it takes time for technologies to be implemented, 
markets are good at discounting expected RoI into equity 
prices. The current tech boom is no exception. Equity markets 
have surged more broadly than the more concentrated GDP 
boost to the tech manufacturers (Figure 5). This pull-forward 
of the expected RoI into asset prices supports consumer 
spending via the wealth effect. Over time, the RoI begins to

alongside healthy wage growth, and the wealth effect rotates 
to income growth in support of consumer spending. Profits 
are cushioned from wage gains as total factor productivity 
reduces unit labor costs. 
Rising interest rates amid the tech boom
Viewing the tech boom through its two stages helps under-
stand why central banks have turned more hawkish despite 
the positive technology shock. This is best understood by 
considering the demand and supply forces that ultimately 
drive inflation and growth (Figure 6).

Stage 1 of the tech boom is a positive demand shock, as both 
capex and consumer spending jump ahead of the benefits of 
the technology that will eventually boost supply. The demand 
curve shifts out. Stage 2 is the positive supply shock that 
comes from the improvements brought by the new technolo-
gy. When this arrives, the supply curve shifts out. Both of 
these stages are influencing central bank policy:
 
1) Cyclical inflation: The first stage of the technology 
shock is a boost in demand ahead of supply, lifting both 
growth and inflation. This is most visible in the surge in 
tech prices. But the broader cyclical lift from robust con-
sumer spending has also boosted the broader pricing com-
plex. With inflation already elevated, this added cyclical 
boost has heightened central bank concerns and is leading 
them to hike rates (Figure 7).

Source: National sources, S&P Global, J.P. Morgan. Details on request.
Tech prices
(ex-China)
Output price PMI
 
2) Rising neutral rates: Behind the cyclical scene is a 
steady drift higher in neutral rates. Rising neutral rates 
often seem at odds with a positive technology shock, 
which is ultimately disinflationary. However, before the 
returns on investments arrive, investment is needed. 
Households that want to spend more via the wealth effect 
need to be incentivized to still save some of their income 
to fund the capex boom. This incentive comes from an 
equilibrium rise in the neutral rate of interest.  Only in the 
long run, after the new technology is installed and produc-
tivity growth slows back down (at a higher level), does 
the neutral rate also move back down. Notably, alongside 
concerns about elevated inflation, central banks (and mar-
kets) have been reassessing neutral rates higher. This 
equilibrium pressure is one reason why this is happening.
One may question why the initial round of capital deepening 
does not in itself boost the supply side of the economy. 
Indeed it does. The discussion around Figure 6 abstracts from 
this initial supply boost. While the supply curve shifts out in 
response to the initial round of capital deepening in the first 
stage of a technology boom, it does so by less than the 
demand boost. In the first stage, the boost to supply comes 
solely from capital deepening, absent the broader multi-factor 
productivity increase. Moreover, the impact of capital on sup-
ply is scaled by capital’s share in output (something that will 
be made more clear in the growth accounting framework 
below). Moreover, the first stage includes the consumer's 
boost to spending from the wealth effect—a behavioral 
response to future expected output with no current supply. 
The combination of these suggests that the first stage of a 
technology boom is characterized by a larger demand shock 
than the initial supply boost. Figure 6 abstracts from the mod-
est supply boost in stage 1. 
A framework for growth and technology
Our US team has argued that the evidence for an AI-lead pro-
ductivity boom is still modest so far (here , here). This rein-
forces our view that that we are still in the stage 1 of the tech 
boom, and that the cyclical lift now underway is encouraging 
businesses to ramp up hiring rather than the byproduct of a 
more structural jobless technology shift. 
The alternative narrative, however, points to the boomy gains 
in US productivity as a strong case that the AI revolution is 
already having economy-wide benefits, suggesting that we 
are already in stage 2 of the tech boom. Central banks have 
begun highlighting the disinflationary forces from stage 2 and

make a case for potentially lower interest rates as a result.4
The US has been at the forefront of the productivity surge, 
reinforcing the case for AI’s role given its rapid development 
of frontier models.  Labor productivity in the US has soared 
2.6%ar over the past 3 years, a sharp step up from the soft 
1.4%ar gains in the last expansion. While it is tempting to 
attribute this to a structural shift, the evidence is less clear. In 
a recent note, we estimated that capital deepening related to 
data centers alone could be accounting for as much as 
0.5%-point to US labor productivity growth. A more careful 
decomposition of productivity growth by the San Francisco 
(SF) Fed goes further and suggests that a multi-factor techno-
logical boost to productivity has yet to arrive.
Consider a generalizable production function that considers 
capital (K) and labor inputs (H for hours worked), rates of uti-
lization of existing capital and effort used in hours worked 
(combined to generate total factor utilization, U), and total 
factor productivity (TFP). This growth accounting offers a 
comprehensive framework, with total GDP (Y) growth given 
as:
where α is the capital share of income and (1 − α) is the labor 
share. The introduction of utilization into this canonical 
growth framework is novel and is based on the work of Basu, 
Fernald, and Kimball (hereafter BFK).5 BFK’s key innova-
tion is to recognize that firms vary the intensity with which 
labor and capital are used (beyond the existing stock or hours 
worked). Because utilization moves systematically with the 
business cycle, failing to account for it incorrectly attributes 
these moves to underlying TFP.
More specifically, measured growth in labor productivity, 
defined as GDP per hour worked (Y/H), can be described as:
Viewed through this lens, labor productivity is driven by three 
factors: 1) capital deepening, which is the growth of capital 
relative to labor, 2) the utilization of capital and effort in 
hours worked, and lastly 3) TFP.
4. See Lisa D. Cook, “AI: A Fed Policymaker’s View,” Federal 
Reserve, July 17, 2025; Christine Lagarde, “The Transformative 
Power of AI,” European Central Bank, April 1, 2025; Andrew 
Bailey, “Global Economic Outlook Panel,” Bank of England, 
October 18, 2025; and Kevin Warsh, “In Our Time,” Federal 
Reserve, August 28, 2026.
5. “Are Technology Improvements Contractionary?” American 
Economic Review, 2006.
Still in stage 1 of the AI tech boom
Multifactor technology shocks boost TFP. That is, they raise 
GDP while still using the same amount of existing inputs. 
Based on the framework above, not all increases in labor pro-
ductivity owe to TFP. In his seminal 1994 Foreign Affairs 
article “The Myth of the Asian Miracle”, Paul Krugman 
argued that the rapid expansion of the East Asian “Tiger” 
economies in the early 1990s was driven primarily by “per-
spiration”—massive capital accumulation and labor mobiliza-
tion—rather than “inspiration” or structural TFP growth. By 
applying standard growth accounting, Krugman likened the 
region’s input-driven trajectory to the Soviet growth model, 
warning that heavy capital deepening without efficiency gains 
faces a diminishing return and a ceiling on growth.

Using this accounting, the SF Fed tracks the decomposition of 
US labor productivity. Their latest estimates tell a different 
story than those suggesting we are already in stage 2 of the 
AI-tech boom (Figure 8). It should come as no surprise that 
the massive capex boom that is well underway is, itself, hav-
ing a significant capital deepening impact on measured labor 
productivity—akin to the Asian “Tigers” of the early 1990s. 
The SF Fed’s estimates suggest that capital deepening 
accounts for roughly 50% of the 2.6%ar jump in US labor 
productivity growth over the past three years. This aligns with 
our US team’s research suggesting that the capex boom, 
including the tech and nontech capital deepening, is adding 
roughly 1 to 1.5%-point to US labor productivity growth 
(here). Less well appreciated is that factor utilization has been 
on the rise, accounting for 20% of the productivity rise. This 
means that true TFP has only accounted for about a third of 
the gain in labor productivity—rising 0.9%ar over the past 
three years. 
The results are even more surprising when looking at the 
momentum over the past two years. Capital deepening has 
continued to soar. But factor utilization has also swung up 
sharply, surging 1.5%ar over the year through 2Q26 after con-
tracting roughly 1%ar as recently as 2023. The implication is 
that TFP has stalled over the past year, a rebuke to the argu-
ment that the AI boom has arrived as a systemic economy-

17
Joseph Lupton (1-212) 834-5735
joseph.p.lupton@jpmorgan.com
JPMorgan Chase Bank NA
Global Economic Research
10 November 2026
J P M O R G A N
wide technology diffusion. Indeed, the AI tech boom has yet 
to generate the sustained TFP gains of the late 1990s.
The jump in TFP in 2018-19 is instructive. This came well 
before the AI boom. The rapid acceleration in TFP over this 
period likely reflects a convergence of late-cycle labor scarci-
ty and a structural wave of enterprise cloud and software 
investment. With unemployment falling below 4% over that 
period, tight labor markets forced firms to eliminate organiza-
tional slack and invest in labor-saving automation, while 
years of heavy intangible capital accumulation began paying 
off in operational efficiency before the pandemic disrupted 
the data. A similar dynamic could have been at work in 2024, 
when tight labor markets and high input costs were forcing 
businesses to find ways to economize.
It is worth a digression on the distinction between capital 
deepening and TFP. A case can be made that some technolog-
ical progress is embodied in capital, an idea that dates to the 
seminal work of Jorgenson and Griliches.6  In this view, the 
current capital-deepening boom is itself installing AI 
technology. The BFK framework does incorporate this 
mechanism by using quality-adjusted capital prices to capture 
improvements embodied in new equipment. In their 
framework, measured multifactor productivity reflects 
disembodied technological change. As such, the argument 
that the tech boom is in the capital deepening cuts both ways. 
If the technology boom is largely a capital-deepening boom, 
then its growth contribution should fade quickly when 
hyperscaler investment slows (akin to the Krugman argument 
about the early 1990s Asian economies). Moreover, it seems 
clear that purchasing AI-related equipment or cloud services 
does not immediately make downstream firms more 
productive, who have yet to even consider let alone develop 
innovative use-cases for the technology (this is the 
multi-factor productivity boost we are still waiting for). 
Installed capital may begin providing services once deployed, 
but realizing its full return requires applications, usable data, 
redesigned workflows, worker training, and other comple-
mentary intangible investments. This is the essence of the 
productivity J-curve outlined by the more optimistic views on 
AI from Brynjolfsson, Rock, and Syverson: measured pro-
ductivity can initially remain weak while firms build the com-
plementary capital needed to exploit the new technology, with 
6. Jorgenson, Dale W., and Zvi Griliches. 1967. “The Explanation of 
Productivity Change.” The Review of Economic Studies 34 (3): 
249–283.
the gains appearing only later.7
Factor utilization also keeps inflation sticky
The innovation of the BFK framework does more than clarify 
thinking about labor productivity. It also serves as a direct 
proxy for the real marginal costs that are central to underlying 
inflation pressures. Because cost-minimizing firms operate 
along all margins, pushing existing workers harder and run-
ning capital equipment continuously requires paying over-
time, accelerating maintenance, and absorbing diminishing 
returns. These intensity adjustments boost costs.
Swings in factor utilization are clear in the data (Figure 9). 
Both manufacturing capacity utilization and average weekly 
hours (used as a direct input to hours but also as a proxy for 
labor effort) move sympathetically over the business cycle. 
Both were depressed from 2022-24 but have begun reversing, 
in line with the total factor utilization measure of BFK 
described above.8

This adds a new wrinkle to the knee-jerk interpretation of 
productivity gains. When rising factor utilization drives a 
large share of top-line labor productivity growth, it signals 
underlying inflationary pressures rather than a tempering 
force on unit labor costs. In modern “New Keynesian” mod-
els of inflation, rising real marginal costs incentivize firms to 
lift their output prices to protect margins. As BFK demon-
strated in their 2006 publication, demand-driven expansions 
that rely heavily on stressing existing factor inputs—rather 
7. Brynjolfsson, Erik, Daniel Rock, and Chad Syverson. 2021. “The 
Productivity J-Curve: How Intangibles Complement General 
Purpose Technologies.” American Economic Journal: 
Macroeconomics 13 (1): 333–372.
8. The BFK study proxies factor utilization with the average 
workweek of labor by industry sectors. The idea that the workweek 
correlates with effort goes back to the labor adjustment cost theory 
of Gary Becker (1965. “A theory of the allocation of time.” The 
Economic Journal). Industrial management literature has gone on to 
show that peak periods of demand tend to come with tighter labor 
monitoring, reducing slack time, and speeding up production lines.

Inflation is driven by many factors. Still, the reasonable align-
ment of factor utilization with US core inflation reinforces its 
role as a real-time proxy for marginal cost (Figure 10). The 
recent jump in factor utilization growth signals that firms are 
using existing labor and capital inputs more intensively, lift-
ing marginal costs. While no single metric is definitive, this 
capacity strain provides further evidence that underlying price 
pressures remain sticky. Combined with resilient growth, this 
cyclical lift argues that central banks (particularly the Fed) 
will face continued pressure to keep policy rates elevated or 
retain a hawkish bias over the coming year.

Just before IEEPA was struck down, the static effective tariff 
rate, weighted by 2024 trade shares, stood at 13.8%, com-
pared to 11.2% today, according to estimates from the Yale 
Budget Lab. However, because importers responded to the 
tariff by changing country sources, the ratio of gross customs 
duties collected to imports was lower, at 11.1% in January 
and 7.1% in September (including the Section 301 tariffs, 
ranging between 10-12.5%, related to forced labor on 60 trad-
ing partners, imposed in July). This was associated with a 
decline in seasonally adjusted, annualized gross customs rev-
enue, from $373bn in January to $280bn in October (Figure 
2).

Budget and inflation effects of new tariffs
Were the administration to fully reconstitute IEEPA tariff lev-
els with the new Section 301 and 232 tariffs, then all the num-
bers above would return to around their IEEPA levels (i.e., an 
effective average tariff rate of close to 14%). The over $90bn 
increase in annual tariff revenue, which is equal to 0.4% of 
consumer spending, would likely be inflationary. But the 
effect would probably be less significant in comparison to the 
first increase in tariff rates in 2025. The reason is that the 
administration has telegraphed all along that tariff rates would 
be going back up. Consequently, there is less incentive than 
there otherwise would be for firms to cut prices in the interim, 
given that they would have to raise them again shortly. 
To put a more precise estimate on this, we leverage a recent 
study by Amiti et al (2026) that estimates how tariff changes 
to date have affected consumer goods prices. That work finds 
that it takes about a year for tariffs to fully pass through, 
though much of the effect occurs within six months. Since the 
end of IEEPA, the authors estimate that the decline in tariff 
rates has reduced the CPI for goods by 0.8%. That would 
imply a 0.9% fall in non-energy goods, assuming the tariff 
changes had little impact on energy, which was largely untar-
iffed, and a 0.2% fall in total core CPI. Although this study 
focused on goods, the implied impact on core inflation from 
goods alone is similar to an earlier study that looked at the 
entire core.

On the day that the Supreme Court struck down the adminis-
tration’s IEEPA tariffs in February, Treasury Secretary Bes-
sent remarked that “Treasury’s estimates show that the use of 
Section 122 authority, combined with potentially enhanced 
Section 232 and Section 301 tariffs will result in virtually 
unchanged tariff revenue in 2026.” USTR Greer likewise 
said, “We are going to recreate the system we have.” Since 
then, however, the tariff rate has hardly budged (Figure 1).  
We expect that is about to change. The administration is like-
ly to begin releasing the results of its Section 301 investiga-
tions into “structural excess capacity” in 16 economies short-
ly, leading to a rebound in tariff rates. The administration is 
also working on additional Section 232 investigations and 
expanding the scope of existing ones (e.g., steel, aluminum, 
and copper products), though their effect on the overall tariff 
rate probably won’t be as material. New tariffs create an addi-
tional inflation impulse, though the effect may be no more 
than a couple tenths of a percent over a year, in part because 
sellers have already anticipated that tariff rates could rise 
again.

Ready or not, here come more

13 November 2026
J P M O R G A N
Assuming a full reversal back to IEEPA tariff rates, and no 
refunds of non-IEEPA tariffs, would thus suggest about a 
0.2% rise in core inflation over a year, with the effects front-
loaded. Alternatively, consider a scenario in which the admin-
istration reverts to IEEPA tariff rates but caps rates at 20%. 
Also assume that the universe of applicable goods stays clos-
er to those being tariffed under the current Section 301 
“forced labor” tariffs, which apply to a narrower universe 
than IEEPA did. We expect this policy would raise the static 
average effective tariff rate to 12.6%.  This would raise core 
inflation by 0.1% instead of 0.2%. 
Another slew of 301 and 232 tariffs
Reconstituting something like IEEPA tariffs will probably 
come from the Section 301 “structural excess capacity” inves-
tigations that are currently underway. While we think these 
get announced soon, it’s possible they don’t come until after 
mid December. That would smooth upcoming meetings 
between Trump and President Xi of China, who are scheduled 
to meet mid November at the APEC meetings in China and 
mid December at the G20 meetings in the US. 
Headline tariff rates could turn out to be higher for some 
economies than under IEEPA, but with delayed implementa-
tion as a means to encourage countries to formalize to trade 
frameworks that had been agreed to under IEEPA. We use the 
term “agreed” loosely, since the US was setting tariff rates 
conditional on these frameworks, but in many cases neither 
the US nor a foreign partner had ever legislated anything. 
Also, with the passage of time, the bilateral trade balances 
have also changed, as have geopolitical relationships. While 
we expect the first round Section 301 tariffs to mimic the 
IEEPA trade deals, they may be reset, and likely higher, for 
some such as ASEAN and the EU. 
There are also Section 232 actions pending: The BIS has pro-
posed adding 14 steel, aluminum, and copper derivative cate-
gories to the tariff schedule, while polysilicon derivatives are 
set to face a 15% tariff from December 4 alongside minimum 
import-price rules. Upholstered furniture is currently subject 
to a 25% tariff, scheduled to rise to 30% on January 1; kitch-
en cabinets and vanities are also at 25%, scheduled to rise to 
50%. Investigations continue into drones, wind turbines, 
pharmaceuticals and medical products, robotics, and industri-
al machinery. 
Separately, the President has threatened 50% tariffs on Cana-
dian autos, trucks, parts, and steel from January 1 if no trade 
deal is reached. Section 301 investigations are also underway 
into Vietnam’s intellectual-property protections and Germa-
ny’s pharmaceutical-pricing policies.
On USMCA, renewal likely doesn’t occur until 2028 or 2029, 
although bilateral frameworks that don’t need to be approved 
by Congress could come sooner, especially for Mexico. The 
mid-January release of USMCA public consultations will be 
the next opportunity to take stock of developments on this 
front. For more, see this recent update from our Mexico col-
leagues. 
Legal challenges galore, and more to come
Several tariff challenges remain unresolved. After the 
Supreme Court invalidated the IEEPA tariffs, refunds are 
underway. Out of an estimated total possible $165bn in 
refunds, about $140bn has been paid back. The pace of repay-
ments has slowed, and the government is opposing repay-
ments to importers that have not applied individually. Last 
May, the Court of International Trade (USCIT) ruled the 10% 
Section 122 tariffs unlawful; its injunction covered only the 
plaintiffs, and the Federal Circuit stayed the ruling pending 
appeal. The tariffs have expired, but potential refunds remain 
disputed. The 50% Section 338 tariff on Canadian imports 
(with broad exemptions) is also being contested on the 
grounds that later laws limit or supersede the 1930 Smoot-
Hawley Act that authorizes it; no hearing date has been 
reported. In contrast, courts have upheld the government in 
several challenges to Section 232 implementation, including 
its extension to derivative products.
Importantly, the recently imposed Section 301 tariffs (10-
12.5% on 60 countries citing forced-labor concerns) have 
been challenged on various counts. These include the admin-
istration’s failure to identify specific foreign practices and 
demonstrate how they burden U.S. commerce, as the law 
requires (and as undertaken for the 2018 China tariffs), and 
that Section 301 is a targeted remedy for unfair trade, not 
authority for a broad tariff regime. The USCIT could rule 
before the end of the year. 
These challenges may take time to resolve, with some, such 
as the Section 301 cases, potentially reaching the Supreme 
Court. In addition, Section 301 excess-capacity tariffs may 
also face lawsuits on similar grounds to those challenging the 
forced-labor duties. In recent cases, courts have ruled based 
on a textual reading of the law: the power to levy tariffs 
belongs to Congress, and any presidential authority to impose 
them must be explicitly delegated through legislation.  If the 
courts continue to apply a similar reading of delegated tariff 
authority, either set of tariffs could be struck down, but reso-
lution may extend well into 2027.
What will Congress do
Many Democrats campaigned against the administration’s tar-
iffs on affordability grounds. After their sweeping victory,

pressure will grow to legislate. To do so, they will need to 
unite on a common tariff policy and contend with the Presi-
dent’s veto power, while the administration can impose new 
tariffs under existing trade laws using executive orders. 
While affordability is a key commitment, Democrats also 
seek to reindustrialize strategic sectors. They have favored 
tax and industrial policies but recognize that tariffs are needed 
to rebuild industries such as shipbuilding and pharmaceuti-
cals. In addition, to avoid presidential vetoes, they will need 
to win enough Republican support or attach tariff measures to 
other bills, forcing the President to choose between accepting 
them or vetoing the broader legislation.
It is therefore likely that tariff-related legislative changes will 
be limited and targeted. Congress could provide certainty dur-
ing the ongoing USMCA renegotiations by temporarily bar-
ring new Section 301 or 232 tariffs on Canada and Mexico 
without its approval. More broadly, the Section 232 tariffs 
could be made subject to Congressional approval, especially 
on the scope of “derivative” products, which the administra-
tion has widened considerably. Congress could also repeal 
Sections 338  and122, which are widely viewed as being out-
dated and exclude specific products from Sections 301 and 
232 on grounds of affordability. More generally, the new 
Congress will likely aim to limit the delegated tariff powers 
of the Executive as currently provided under U.S. trade acts.

APEC 2026: Reducing the cost of 
fragmentation
12 November 2026
J P M O R G A N
From integration to resilience
The distinction from earlier APEC meetings is the ambition 
and pragmatism: reduce the cost of fragmentation rather than 
assume integration will deepen. Predictable clearance sup-
ports shipments; durable access and bankable projects support 
factories and infrastructure. Cooperation is most plausible 
where members can share those benefits without surrendering 
security restrictions.
Past achievements set concrete benchmarks. Shanghai’s 2001 
Accord targeted a 5% reduction in trade transaction costs over 
five years; APEC subsequently estimated a further 5% reduc-
tion between 2006 and 2010, with US$58.7bn in savings. 
Vladivostok’s 2012 agreement covered 54 environmental-
goods categories and tariffs of 5% or less by end-2015, fol-
lowing product-level consultations that continued into summit 
week. Beijing’s 2014 FTAAP roadmap and 2015–2025 Con-
nectivity Blueprint supplied direction, even without a trade 
agreement. The lesson for APEC in 2026 is to narrow the 
scope and specify implementation: customs digitization, stan-
dards cooperation and infrastructure financing are more plau-
sible than broad tariff liberalization.
Founded days before the Berlin Wall fell in 1989, APEC rein-
forced globalization that supported China’s industrialization 
and post-WTO integration (Figure 1), alongside domestic 
reform, binding market access and corporate investment. Its 
agenda has broadened from liberalization to connectivity, 
resilience and inclusive growth. Today, impaired WTO appel-
late review weakens enforcement, while tariff wars and secu-
rity restrictions make access more conditional. APEC can 
preserve cooperation across competing regional rulebooks; it 
cannot replace enforceable multilateral rules.

FTAAP remains a vision, not a treaty under negotiation. Pro-
posed by the APEC Business Council in 2004 and taken up 
by leaders in 2006, it was advanced through the 2014 road-
map, 2016 study and 2024 renewal; the APEC Ministers 
Responsible for Trade Meeting in Suzhou on 22–23 May

APEC 2026: Openness under pressure
The 33rd APEC Economic Leaders’ Meeting in Shenzhen on 
18–19 November, alongside the CEO Summit on 17–18, con-
fronts tariff walls, supply-chain disruption and relocation, AI 
rivalry, tech and rare-earth controls, energy vulnerability and 
weak household demand. The theme, “Building an Asia-Pa-
cific Community to Prosper Together,” and priorities of 
“openness, innovation and cooperation” address a central 
contradiction: regional economies depend on Chinese manu-
facturing, US demand and capital, and Asian technology net-
works while increasingly restricting those links.
APEC remains an economic forum, with voluntary commit-
ments reached by consensus. This favors three results: trade 
facilitation, investable AI–energy cooperation and stronger 
domestic-demand policy coordination. September’s APEC 
Energy Ministerial Meeting and October’s APEC Finance 
Ministers’ Meeting provide the negotiating foundation, 
including open trade corridors, digital-infrastructure financing 
and household resilience. Leaders can set direction and assign 
follow-through; tariff concessions, funding and commercial 
contracts require separate decisions. U.S. participation is 
essential to meaningful renewal, but a regional trade agree-
ment or common fiscal package is unlikely.
The CEO Summit will test whether policy cooperation can 
attract commercial commitments. Planned sessions cover 
connectivity, the Free Trade Area of the Asia-Pacific 
(FTAAP), AI and the digital–green transition; the Greater Bay 
Area session connects Shenzhen’s industrial networks with 
Hong Kong’s financial and professional services. Production 
and financing partnerships would offer evidence of business 
confidence, but the published agenda does not establish con-
tracts or market-access concessions.
APEC 2026: Reducing the 
cost of fragmentation

J P M O R G A N
tion. Existing FTAs benchmark FTAAP’s gains and dilution 
risks: the Regional Comprehensive Economic Partnership 
(RCEP) offers tariff preferences and common origin rules; the 
Comprehensive and Progressive Agreement for Trans-Pacific 
Partnership (CPTPP), excluding China and the US, adds 
deeper state-enterprise, labor and environmental disciplines. 
Bilateral FTAs may offer better product-specific terms. 
FTAAP could broaden US–China coverage and simplify 
overlapping services, digital and origin rules, but accommo-
dating divergent systems risks weaker commitments and wid-
er exceptions. Its value depends on expanding access without 
diluting standards.
We expect the Summit to advance FTAAP work program 
rather than launch treaty negotiations. At August’s Senior 
Officials’ Meeting, China proposed a standalone FTAAP out-
come alongside connectivity, AI and digital-economy deliver-
ables; consensus and implementation are essential. Standards 
interoperability and existing capacity building could reduce 
overlapping compliance costs and improve investment pre-
dictability, not establishing new tariff preferences, cross-
agreement origin cumulation, or substantial market opening.
Trade: Continuity, not liberalization
The strongest trade prospect is procedural certainty. The 2026 
APEC Ministers Responsible for Trade Joint Statement 
(Suzhou Statement), issued on 23 May, reaffirmed capacity 
building; the APEC Customs–Business Dialogue in Dalian in 
August 2026 addressed paperless trade and electronic certifi-
cation. In Shenzhen, leaders could identify participating econ-
omies and set review milestones for this work, supporting 
shorter clearance times and lower working-capital costs. Sep-
arately, a US–China truce extension and implemented relief 
could protect selected access, without automatically extend-
ing concessions to other members.
For China, October trade remained strong despite slower 
export momentum, underscoring the growth cushion that 
APEC cooperation can help preserve (Figure 2). Exports rose 
35.2%oya, following 36.3% in September; October’s 1.0%m/
m sa decline only partly reversed September’s 6.5% surge. 
October imports rose 6.5%m/m sa and 41.6%oya, narrowing 
the surplus from US$133.5bn to US$105.3bn. We see the 
full-year 2026 surplus on track for around US$1.3tr. Technol-
ogy and commodity prices amplify nominal trade values, lim-
iting the inference for real growth. Preserving access supports 
external absorption, but procedural gains cannot offset 
renewed tariffs or weak domestic demand.

Regional effects run through both Chinese sales and purchas-
es. September exports to EM Asia rose 8.0%m/m sa, to the 
US 5.9% and Japan 2.7%; October imports from Australia 
rose 15.0%m/m sa, the US 8.6%, Japan 7.9% and EM Asia 
4.9%. Energy restocking and technology inputs led the import 
rebound, not household spending. More reliable clearance 
would benefit US buyers, Japanese and Korean suppliers, 
ASEAN factories and Australian exporters, while retaining 
competitive pressure from China’s manufacturing upgrade. 
These flows demonstrate interdependence, though not pro-
spective gains attributable to APEC.
Purchase deals offer a narrower channel: Chinese orders 
could benefit US agriculture, energy and industry while dis-
placing Australian or other regional sales. The test is addi-
tional demand rather than redirected trade. ASEAN factories 
serving U.S. customers with Chinese inputs would gain more 
from predictable access and transparent origin procedures; 
stricter scrutiny would continue to constrain simple rerouting.
Investment gains require more than a temporary truce. Dia-
logue on approvals and screening is more plausible than an 
APEC-wide guarantee; reciprocal access remains a separate 
negotiation. Predictable operating rules could support Chi-
nese localization abroad and foreign investment in China’s 
supplier clusters and market. Localization preserves custom-
ers and earnings but shifts some employment and value added 
overseas; ASEAN hosts gain most when local procurement 
and skills deepen. Short-lived relief primarily supports ship-
ments and inventories, not long-payback factories.
Our earlier analysis makes the strategic point: export diversi-
fication and shifting semiconductor networks do not eliminate 
shared policy, geopolitical and financial risks. APEC’s contri-
bution would be to keep these networks commercially work-
able, reducing delays and duplication. Redistribution of pro-
duction across rival camps does not necessarily result in an 
increase in regional prosperity.

APEC 2026: Reducing the cost of

AI: From ambition to investment
AI offers the clearest link between APEC negotiations and 
investment delivery. The 2026 APEC Digital and AI Ministe-
rial Statement (Chengdu, 23 July) reaffirmed the 2026–2030 
AI Initiative; the Finance Ministers’ Policy Statement on Dig-
ital Infrastructure Financing Across the Asia-Pacific Region 
(Hong Kong, 21 October) identifies project preparation, first-
loss guarantees and risk sharing. Leaders could prioritize 
cooperation among governments, investors and multilateral 
development banks around bankable projects. J.P. Morgan 
Global Research projects roughly US$900bn of global AI 
capex in 2026, driven substantially by US hyperscalers; much 
of the 2027 pipeline is already funded or under construction. 
APEC can ease financing bottlenecks in this cycle, yet a com-
mon fund or technology-access bargain is unlikely.
Power constraints provide the practical AI–energy bridge. 
The APEC Policy Recommendations on the Mutual Enhance-
ment between AI and Energy in the Asia-Pacific 
(2026–2030), adopted at the APEC Energy Ministerial Meet-
ing (Beijing, 10–11 September 2026), cover coordinated com-
puting–grid planning, efficiency benchmarks and pilots in 
load forecasting and energy management. We expect imple-
mentation priorities under the proposed Energy Working 
Group task force, rather than binding regional standards. Fast-
er connections and reliable power could support US hyper-
scalers, Taiwan’s foundries and packaging, Korean memory, 
Japanese equipment and ASEAN computing and electronics. 
China could gain grid, battery and optical-component orders, 
while chip controls and data rules continue to segment access.
The impact would be concentrated in investment execution 
and supplier demand. China’s October data-processing equip-
ment exports rose 5.4%m/m sa despite falling chip and phone 
exports; high-tech import values rose 2.5%, the twelfth con-
secutive monthly gain. Infrastructure cooperation could sup-
port these orders without reopening U.S.-led tech networks. 
Imported equipment offsets much of data-center equipment 
spending’s direct US GDP contribution, leaving Asian and 
Mexican production more exposed to a slowdown. U.S. risks 
also operate through asset prices and financial conditions. 
Progress requires permits, grid connections and secured 
financing ready for disbursement, not endorsements alone.
Energy: Resilience without lock-in
Energy negotiations favor supply resilience over common 
guarantees. The September APEC Energy Ministerial Meet-
ing endorsed producer–consumer coordination and economy-
specific mixes of conventional and low-emission energy; 
leaders could prioritize diversification and infrastructure plan-
ning. J.P. Morgan research reports Middle Eastern exports 
recovering to 20.7mbd from March’s 9.8mbd low, but still 
below the pre-war 24.7mbd. Crude inventories are rebuilding 
in China and Korea, but refined-product stocks remain near 
seasonal lows. For regional importers, restored crude flows 
do not eliminate fuel-supply risks; APEC cooperation should 
address delivery and refining bottlenecks as well as sourcing.
Commercial deals on the sidelines could complement this 
agenda. U.S. oil and LNG and Australian supply could diver-
sify regional sourcing; APEC itself would set neither volumes 
nor prices. China’s October crude import volumes rose 
4.6%m/m sa but remained 13.3% below a year earlier; 
refined-product volumes rebounded 74.0%m/m sa after Sep-
tember’s 17.5% decline. Further crude restocking is expected, 
constrained by weak domestic fuel demand. Flexible supply 
terms would improve resilience without converting a tempo-
rary shock into excessive long-term commitments.
For the transition, the summit could prioritize grid and stor-
age demonstrations and technical exchanges under the AI–
energy recommendations, supported by the October APEC 
Finance Ministers’ Meeting’s project-preparation tools. Chi-
nese renewables, batteries, grids and electric transport, Japa-
nese and Korean engineering, and US and Asian capital could 
lower regional fuel intensity and transition costs. Financing 
agreements remain separate. Gas provides flexibility but 
retains import exposure; renewables require grids and firm-
ing. Projects should reflect efficiency gains and economy-
specific carbon-neutral pathways to limit fuel lock-in and 
stranded assets.
The economic dividend for the region would be reliable pow-
er and lower disruption costs. The September 2026 APEC 
AI–energy policy recommendations also seek to ensure that 
computing expansion does not shift energy costs onto house-
holds and other industries. Capacity announcements alone 
would not establish either benefit.
Rebalancing: Turning output into demand
Rebalancing could become the summit’s most important poli-
cy signal, but its least immediate economic result. October’s 
Finance Ministers meeting recognized a common interest in 
global rebalancing and produced a household-support and 
human-capital report and a financial-access compendium. 
Leaders could endorse implementation reviews and continued 
policy exchanges. This would connect openness with stronger 
domestic absorption, without committing members to com-
mon spending targets or coordinated stimulus.
China illustrates why this matters. September high-tech man-
ufacturing output rose 15.3%oya and industrial production 
5.5% in real terms, while nominal retail sales grew 1.1%, 
consistent with a persistent production–absorption gap. Octo-
ber producer-goods prices rose 7.4%oya, consumer-goods 

25
Feng Zhu (852) 2800 1745
feng.zhu@jpmorgan.com
JPMorgan Chase Bank, N.A., Hong Kong Branch
Global Economic Research
12 November 2026
J P M O R G A N
prices fell 0.1% and core CPI eased to 0.8%; energy drove 
more than 80% of the unadjusted monthly PPI increase. Cost 
pressure is not demand-led reflation. China’s upgrading low-
ers partners’ equipment costs but intensifies competition for 
Japanese, Korean and U.S. producers. Stronger Chinese con-
sumption would expand the demand side of that relationship.
The October APEC Finance Ministers’ Joint Statement’s 
agenda on household support, human capital and financial 
access provides a framework, not a Chinese policy commit-
ment. Our analysis of China’s consumption deficit identifies 
weak transmission from productivity gains to household 
income, compounded by property losses, employment insecu-
rity and precautionary saving. Transfers, social protection, 
housing completion and private-service jobs could improve 
that transmission; further industrial investment alone would 
not. APEC can support policy learning and financial access, 
but stronger purchasing power depends on domestic funding 
and execution. Financial inclusion should complement 
income support, not replace it with household borrowing.
Implementation would broaden markets for U.S. agriculture 
and services, Japanese and Korean consumer businesses, 
ASEAN tourism and food, and Australian resources and agri-
culture. Human-capital investment and local supplier devel-
opment would also spread relocation gains. Purchase pledges 
and currency changes cannot substitute for stronger incomes 
and capabilities. The likely immediate APEC result is a 
shared agenda; the lasting dividend requires production and 
investment gains to reach households.
Geopolitics: A truce, not a reset
A Trump–Xi meeting, if held, would likely seek a truce 
extension beyond 10 January 2027, implementation of pro-
posed tariff relief and verifiable purchase and supply commit-
ments. The May and September Trump–Xi summits—with 
trade and investment boards and subsequent procedural 
advances—provide the framework. Proposed relief covers 
roughly US$30bn of non-sensitive goods each way at 2024 
trade values—not savings or new orders—and remains unver-
ified in implementation. Quarterly reviews and delivery dates 
would make an extension more credible, protecting Chinese 
access and regional production networks. These would be 
bilateral results alongside APEC, not regional concessions.
Technology discussions could pair eligible chip approvals 
with dependable critical-mineral licensing without reversing 
strategic controls. The Bureau of Industry and Security (BIS) 
permits conditional case-by-case review of H200 and 
MI325X chips; economic benefits still require licenses and 
deliveries. GE’s more than US$12.9bn of November sales and 
service signings illustrate the commercial space that remains. 
Its separate spare-engine package includes C919-compatible 
LEAP-1C engines, but the total is not C919-specific. Fulfilled 
contracts would support aviation supply chains, not establish 
broader technology liberalization.
US domestic politics favors tangible commercial gains over 
sensitive concessions. Incoming Democratic control of both 
chambers in January strengthens oversight without removing 
presidential discretion. Nvidia-related scrutiny adds pressure 
for chip-export oversight, tracking and tighter allied equip-
ment controls; proposals are not enacted restrictions or find-
ings of wrongdoing. Beijing needs usable licenses and review 
mechanisms, not personal assurances. Investors should distin-
guish lower near-term trade uncertainty from durable technol-
ogy access.
The possible Trump–Xi–Putin dialogue adds a conditional 
security upside. The meeting remains unconfirmed; Trump 
has signaled Iran and denuclearization discussions; Ukraine is 
a reported Russian priority, but not an agreed agenda item. 
We expect exploratory diplomacy rather than a comprehen-
sive settlement. “Yalta 2.0” describes the perceived stakes, 
not an agreed framework or authority to settle conflicts with-
out the affected countries. Credible de-escalation could lower 
regional energy, freight and insurance costs, but would not 
automatically lift sanctions. Executable trade commitments 
should remain separable from a broader security bargain.
Standing together requires delivery
Our central expectation is limited renewal: practical trade 
facilitation, AI–energy implementation priorities and stronger 
recognition of domestic-demand rebalancing. A longer US–
China truce would be the most consequential possible bilater-
al outcome. The upside is commitments that reach approvals, 
projects and deliveries; the downside is a declaration without 
operational follow-through.
Reduced disruption would protect China’s export cushion, US 
supply certainty, Japanese and Korean orders, ASEAN invest-
ment execution and Australian outlets. Infrastructure coopera-
tion could ease bottlenecks, while domestic rebalancing could 
broaden demand over time. For investors, implemented relief 
can improve operating visibility; stronger, sustainable earn-
ings require delivered investment and demand, not diplomatic 
confidence alone.
Standing together requires workable regional links and shared 
gains. Export strength buys time, not rebalancing; announced 
investment is not productive capacity. The APEC summit 
may reduce fragmentation costs, but is unlikely to arrest the 
broader trend while tariff barriers, technology controls and 
geopolitical rivalry persist.

Resilience has been unevenly spread
Before the pandemic, employment growth across the core 
10 and periphery 11 broadly followed a common cycle. Since 
2023, however, their paths have increasingly diverged. By

That divergence is increasingly visible in unemployment. 
Core job creation has been too weak to absorb even modest 
labour-force growth, pushing the unemployment rate to 5.8%, 
around 0.5%-pt above its end-2023 level. In contrast, much 
stronger employment growth has more than absorbed the 
larger expansion in labour supply in the periphery, where 
unemployment has fallen 1.5%-pt to 7.3%, close to a record 
low (Figure 3). The stable Euro area unemployment rate 
therefore increasingly masks a loosening in the core, where 
the unemployment rate has historically been lower.
The sectoral picture reinforces this divide (Figure 4). Core 
weakness began in manufacturing, where employment peaked 
in 1H23 amid growing competitiveness challenges, the linger-
ing effects of the 2022 energy shock and tighter financial con-
ditions. It subsequently spread to construction and services. 
Public-sector hiring has provided a partial offset, but not

3Q26 9 (Figure 1), prompting us to lower our 2H26 forecast

Beneath the aggregate slowdown, a persistent two-speed 
labour market remains in place. Employment growth has 
remained relatively firm in the periphery, but employment has 
been contracting in the core, where Germany remains the 
main drag, while French labour demand has also softened.

employment growth looking still decent, but that cushion is 
now narrowing as hiring momentum outside the core also 
slows.

But the periphery has now lost some momentum. Incoming 
3Q data point to slower hiring, including in Spain, where 
immigration-fuelled job creation has been a key source of 
Euro area employment resilience in recent years (Figure 1). 
As that support fades, the aggregate pick-up increasingly 
depends on labour demand turning higher in the core.
Germany remains the main core drag
The employment slowdown has been largely broad-based 
across the core, including the two largest economies, France 
and Germany (Figure 5). In France, employment has moved 
sideways, but this has been largely in line with the disappoint-
ing activity, but Germany is where the disconnect between 
resilient activity and weak labour demand is striking. Indeed, 
employment has continued to decline at a modest but persis-
tent pace despite the recovery in activity since mid-2025 (Fig-
ures 5 and 6).

Relative to Germany’s pre-pandemic relationship between 
employment and output, this underperformance is highly 
unusual. As Figure 5 shows, annual GDP growth of around 
1% would historically have been associated with employment 
growth of roughly 0.9%, rather than the 0.5% contraction cur-
rently observed (Figure 6) 12. The fall in employment has 
instead allowed the recovery in output to feed entirely into 
productivity. This marks a sharp reversal from the earlier 
period of labour hoarding, when firms absorbed weak output 
through lower working hours and diminished productivity 
rather than lower headcount.

Importantly, the breadth of the weakness suggests that it can-
not simply be explained by a shift towards less labour-inten-
sive sectors, nor is it confined to manufacturing. Employment 
has contracted across several industries that had supported the 
recovery since mid-2025, including manufacturing, where 
activity has since returned to growth, as well as ICT and retail 
(Figure 7). Instead, we think the weakness still partly reflects 
a delayed and slow adjustment to earlier labour hoarding. 
Firms entered the recovery with excess labour relative to out-
put and have been able to accommodate stronger activity 
12. We note that this low elasticity of employment to GDP has 
historically made German productivity highly pro-cyclical: 
productivity falls when growth is subdued but picks up strongly 
when the economy expands at a robust pace, as in the pre-pandemic 
period.

Looking ahead, the near-term signal remains weak. Capacity 
utilisation is subdued, vacancies have yet to recover and fur-
lough usage remains elevated. The employment PMI also 
continued to signal private-sector job losses through 3Q26, 
although aggregate employment has performed somewhat 
better because resilient public-sector hiring falls outside the 
PMI universe (Figure 8). Nevertheless, we take some comfort 
from the directional improvement in the survey, which points 
to a moderating pace of private-sector job losses in 4Q. We 
expect that improvement to continue as economic growth 
accelerates to around 1.75%ar this quarter. The headcount 
adjustment should then gradually run its course, with job loss-
es fading through year-end, employment stabilising in 1Q27 
and modest growth of around 0.25%ar resuming from 2Q27.

Labour-demand recovery in the core is key
Our expectation that German job losses will fade and French 
hiring will soon recover underpins our view that Euro area 
employment growth likely bottomed in 3Q26. We expect 
growth to rebound to around 0.3%ar in 4Q26 before firming 
to 0.5%ar from 1Q27 as the energy shock fades and activity 
strengthens. The latest Euro area PMI employment tracker 
supports the near-term direction of our call, pointing to 
growth of around 0.9%ar (Figure 9). However, the tracker has 
been materially stronger than the official data in recent quar-
ters. The source and persistence of this gap remain unclear, as 
does whether it will close through stronger realised employ-
ment growth, a weaker survey signal or both. Conservatively 
allowing for the tracker’s recent tendency to run ahead of the 
official data, a sustained October reading would still be con-
sistent with employment growth around our 0.3%ar forecast. 
We acknowledge, however, that while the survey supports a 
near-term bounce, it does not yet confirm that a durable 
recovery is underway.

In summary, we think Euro area employment growth proba-
bly bottomed in 3Q and should firm modestly from 4Q, but 
the conditions for a durable recovery are not yet in place. A 
sustained improvement requires labour demand in the core to 
recover and the much-needed regional handover to materia-
lise. Until then, weak labour demand remains a downside risk 
to the outlook.
Weak job gains are dampening labour income growth, while a 
delayed recovery would weigh further on the expected pick-
up from 2027. Recent income data provide only partial reas-
surance. Although nominal income growth has picked up, 
much of that improvement has come from financial and trans-
fer income, whose persistence is uncertain and whose support 
for consumption may be less reliable than broad-based gains 
in labour income. Meanwhile, growth in compensation of 
employees, although more resilient than expected, continued 
to moderate. Deteriorating employment prospects could also 
weigh on confidence and encourage precautionary saving. 
The latter is also important because our consumption forecast 
for the upcoming quarters assumes that households continue 
to reduce their saving rate.
