Does this mean that the fiscal easing is now likely to be even 
larger than previously projected? No, this is not the case, in 
our view. First, the draft budgetary plan is based on outdated 
growth forecasts, which the government itself revised up 
shortly after its publication. The updated GDP forecasts 
would produce a faster reduction in the cyclical part of the 
deficit. Second, the forecast of the GDP deflator looks low, 
with only 2.3%oya projected for 2026. An updated view on 
this would increase the level of nominal GDP and hence low-
er the debt ratio. Third, nominal GDP itself has been revised 
up for prior quarters by the German statistics office, which 
gives a lower starting point for the debt ratio in 2025. Fourth, 
the large increase in the debt ratio in 2028 likely incorporates 
a large stock-flow adjustment of almost 1% of GDP, which 
lifts the debt ratio relative to the contribution from the deficit. 
The government has predicted large stock-flow adjustments 
in the past that were later on reduced or removed altogether. 
To illustrate the impact of these assumptions, the debt ratio in 
2028 may be up to 4%-pt lower at closer to 68.5% if we use 
the government’s latest GDP projections and make the other 
corrections listed above. 
Separately, the government has also increased the deficit path 
in the latest draft federal 2027 budget (Table 3). The prior 
plan was published only a few months ago. The changes are 
not very large, but the direction is to larger deficits.

German fiscal policy remains a key part of our macro fore-
casts, but tracking its delivery remains notoriously difficult. 
German GDP growth has picked up over the past year as the 
general government deficit has widened to around 4% of 
GDP in 1H26, and the German government has increased its 
budget deficit projections. At the same time, there have been 
some shortfalls in the planned debt issuance for defence and 
infrastructure investment, and the composition of the GDP 
growth pickup does not show a very clear fiscal impact. The 
political debate on reforms has also not seen a sustained 
improvement. Despite this mixed picture, a large fiscal easing 
is on track. Table 1 restates our largely unchanged view of a 
2% of GDP fiscal easing over 2026 and 2027 (via the change 
in the structural primary balance).

Still throwing big numbers around
Germany’s fiscal shift is highly consequential, not only for 
the near-term GDP growth but also for facilitating a longer-
term structural improvement in the German economy and as a 
driver of the European bond market. The various documents 
produced by the German government are, however, often dif-
ficult to assess. They typically focus on the federal budget 
rather than the broader general government budget and can 
use outdated macroeconomic assumptions even when they are 
published. 
The draft budgetary plan, which is submitted to the European 
Commission each October, is the main document to project 
the general government budget on a National Accounts basis, 
rather than only the federal budget on a cash basis. The latest

the fiscal shift is on track

Budget data tracking in the other direction
As noted above, the general budget deficit widened to 4% of 
GDP in 1H26 on a seasonally adjusted basis. The 4.6% deficit 
in the draft budgetary plan for 2026 would require a sharp 
further widening in 2H26, which is unlikely. 
The monthly federal budgetary outturns are pointing in the 
same direction. Through September, the year-to-date federal 
deficit has increased to €64.2bn, versus a full-year plan of 
€98bn (Figure 1). The planned federal debt issuance is also 
€98bn but only €21bn has been issued to date, which is an 
even larger shortfall. The debt issued for the Special Fund for 
Infrastructure is also running short of the target for 2026 after 
a €13bn shortfall seen already last year. Monthly data on 
defence spending, both from the core budget and the Special 
Fund for the Army, are even further behind the plan.

On track in a broader sense
All of this raises questions about the widening of the general 
government deficit, which has run ahead of the moves in the 
cash deficit and especially of the defence and infrastructure 
categories. 
The recently published assessments of the German institutes 
and the Council of Economic Advisors show German deficit 
forecasts very close to our own at just above 4% in 2026 and 
2027, and a slower increase in the debt ratio than in the gov-
ernment’s draft budgetary plan. Hence, they are also taking 
the government’s documents with a pinch of salt, but that still 
leaves a large fiscal easing of around 2% of GDP. 
The final GDP report for 3Q26 later this month will provide 
an update on the general government deficit and also show if 
higher public investment spending is showing up more clearly 
in the data. The large spikes in domestic manufacturing 
orders, which reflect the upfront recording of new orders (e.g. 
in defence), are an indication that large orders have been 
placed (Figure 3). This has already boosted the backlog of 
domestic orders in the “other transport equipment” category, 
which includes many defence goods (Figure 4). Over time, 
this should show up in the capex data. The government’s 
dashboard on the Special Fund for Infrastructure also now has 
a rapidly growing list of specific projects. Many are in the 
planning stage, which points to possible delays rather than a 
shortfall in delivery. In our view, the political pressure to 
deliver improvements in infrastructure remains large.

The debate in Germany around the fiscal shift remains com-
plicated, however. The focus is much less on the fiscal easing, 
but on the sustainability of the medium-term fiscal path, with 
significant consolidation required after the initial easing and a 
significant number of economic reforms. The government is 
also struggling to find funding for promised tax cuts and 
spending increases, even as past outturns are falling short. 
This confuses the debate, even as a large shift is underway.

Elsewhere, tightening is a response to upside growth surpris-
es, consistent with an apparent increase in the neutral rate 
(r*). It has also been gradual, balancing inflation/unemploy-
ment risks, and reflecting some optimism on global disinfla-
tion. The RBA by contrast is downgrading its assessment of 
capacity as inflation and unemployment jointly rise. Here we 
outline the RBA’s approach, and sketch out the implications 
using Taylor Rules. 
Show me your potential (Y*, U*)
The output gap benchmarks GDP to its trend (Y*). An output 
gap derived from simple de-trending of Australian GDP is 
near zero, suggesting capacity pressure has been relatively 
neutral lately (Figure 3). RBA officials, however, argue that 
potential output has in fact deteriorated more than is reflected 
in GDP outcomes, meaning a positive output gap. At the start 
of the year, Governor Bullock argued that “the level of 
demand is too high”, and in tightening this month, “growth 
will need to remain subdued and the labour market will need 
to ease further to bring overall demand and supply back into 
balance and inflation sustainably back to target.”

Weak productivity is the natural scapegoat for lower potential 
output. Other things equal, this should mean that slower GDP 
growth doesn’t raise unemployment to the same extent. But 
unemployment has risen 0.7%-pt this year, faster than RBA 
forecasts assumed. With respect to inflation, the leadership 
squares this by carrying the weaker capacity argument 
through to the labour market as well. In last month’s Senate 
testimony, Governor Bullock stated that “the labour market 
seems a little tighter than we had previously judged. The evi-
dence for this is elevated underlying inflation.” The staff 
raised its view on NAIRU in the SoMP, citing “underlying 
inflation and labour cost growth have been persistently ele-
vated.” 
NAIRU: If it walks like a duck...
The RBA has a dual mandate: an inflation target band (2-3%) 
alongside a full employment objective. Full employment is a 
long-run concept which applies after all shocks have faded 
•
The RBA is increasingly inferring capacity (Y*, U*) 
from inflation outcomes...
•
...while its actions suggest the intent to be restrictive 
rather than tracing a rising r*
•
The approach contrasts with other central banks 
which have so far erred toward supply-side optimism 
and not actively sought slack, or a restrictive stance
•
The RBA’s approach means greater sensitivity to glob-
al inflation outcomes on the way down too
The RBA has lost patience with above-target inflation this 
year (Figure 1). The board began tightening in February, then 
in a particularly hawkish initial response to the Middle East 
conflict, raised the cash rate further in the early days of the oil 
supply shock.

out. The NAIRU, which we will take to be U*, is the opera-
tionally-relevant proxy for a central bank in the shorter-run, 
defining the level of unemployment above which inflation is 
expected to fall. NAIRU assumptions are then revealing as to 
the central bank’s assumptions about supply-side impulses 
and willingness to lean against inflation of all kinds. 
Figure 4 shows a simple NAIRU measure derived from the 
data, which is agnostic as to whether inflation is driven by the 
labour market directly, or by supply shocks/other noise.13 
Naturally, as the economy experiences both higher inflation 
and higher unemployment under recent supply shocks, this 
measure has been going up. This formalizes the risk that 
higher inflation outcomes become embedded in inflation 
expectations/pricing behaviour.

U*, including supply

component
As revealed in a recent Freedom of Information (FoI) release, 
the RBA staff had been fading such models, which like Figure 
4 had been mechanically rising with realized inflation. The 
technical NAIRU assumption used in the SoMP has been 
lower, respecting the evidence from broader measures of 
labour market capacity and easing labour cost growth. That 
changed over the last year, as just before the oil shock in Feb-
ruary, the staff were arguing for “removing some of the exist-
ing downward judgement on NAIRU”. From a “narrative per-
spective” this would help in “centralizing upside inflation 
risks”, particularly with respect to inflation expectations. The 
staff’s NAIRU estimate rose from 4.5% to 4.6%, and again in 
November to 4.75%, essentially in line with current unem-
ployment. 
13. See Ball and Mankiw (2002). This uses a simple specification of 
the Phillips Curve: Inflation(t) - Inflation(t-1) = a[U*-U(t)] + S(t), 
where a is the absolute value of the Phillips Curve slope, and S(t) 
jointly represents supply, inflation expectations and other shocks. 
We use the inflation and unemployment data, along with RBA 
estimates of a to back out U* + S(t)/a = U(t) + [Inflation(t) - 
Inflation(t-1)]/a. This identifies NAIRU, up to the unobserved S(t) 
term. A central bank which assumes persistence in this term is 
collapsing it into the definition of U*. 
The leadership’s narrative has evolved similarly. In the Sep-
tember minutes, the board thought it prudent to  “reinforce the 
anchoring” of inflation expectations “even if part of the medi-
um-term persistence in inflation is driven by factors beyond 
the influence of monetary policy”. In other words, even if 
inflation is not emanating directly from the labour market, 
higher unemployment may nevertheless be required to neu-
tralize it. Operationally, this is a higher U*.
Assume we have a can opener
As discussed separately, the increase in Australian inflation 
over the last year is largely goods/energy-driven, and counter 
to the direction of the labour market. As would be expected 
under energy shocks, inflation is higher than labour market 
proxies would suggest (Figure 5). The economy appears to 
have been knocked off its Phillips Curve by global inflation. 
If expectations are well-anchored, inflation will return to this 
relationship, though RBA leadership has expressed concern 
that persistent supply shocks may dislodge expectations.

Leaning against supply-side inflation/expectations shocks to 
this extent stands in contrast to other central banks which, so 
far, see stronger demand and are yet more optimistic on the 
outlook on disinflation. Fed Chair Warsh has rejected the 
“cruel choice between full employment and stable prices”, 
seemingly not even worrying about demand-driven inflation. 
And he seems to endorse the view that AI advancements 
might improve policy trade-offs from the supply side. Fed 
Governor Waller pleaded in September to “give disinflation a 
chance”, and even in turning more hawkish this week, did so 
with the backing of a “solid” labour market. The Bank of 
England had been squeamish about tightening despite labour 
market resilience, hiking for the first time only last week. 
The RBNZ has faced the same deviation from its inflation tar-
get as the RBA, but rather than infer capacity pressure from 
inflation, asserts slack as a given, and uses this to justify why 
above-inflation won’t persist: “The economy continues to 
operate below capacity….it could be less likely that short-
term price pressures driven by supply-side shortages will

become embedded in medium-term inflation.”
In making different assumptions about the drivers of inflation 
and persistence of supply shocks, central banks have different 
stakes in the outcome. Were oil prices to continue trending 
lower, those which had swung hard like the RBA would see a 
rapid shift in the balance of risks on inflation vs unemploy-
ment. By contrast, those which had prioritized labour markets 
all along may not feel much reduction in pressure, as a fading 
supply shock would further boost growth, so not fully resolv-
ing the inflation issue. 
Neutral rates (r*)
The inherently harder stance shown by the RBA is recognized 
by the staff/leadership, in their depiction of financial condi-
tions. In September, board members “considered the tentative 
signs that the neutral rate in Australia had again shifted high-
er”, but the Bank ultimately dismissed any dramatic recent 
changes. Growth and unemployment haven’t followed the 
global resilience template, and interest-sensitive variables, 
particularly in housing and credit, are weak (Figure 6).
The staff’s neutral rate suite in the November SoMP was 
essentially unmoved, averaging in the mid-3s for the semi-
structural models, and around 4% for those with more time-
varying parameters. Today’s cash rate of 4.85% is then clearly 
restrictive relative to the models, which synchs with the data.

Even on AI capex, central to the higher r* narrative globally, 
Governor Bullock’s last press conference emphasized its 
import-intensity, with growth downgraded despite more 
upbeat business investment assumptions. The SoMP noted “if 
the improvement in growth expectations is concentrated 
abroad, such as from AI-related investments, Australia may 
import some of the rise in long-term yields without receiving 
the same benefit of higher expected growth.” 
In acknowledging that the higher r* theme doesn’t apply as 
well locally, but tightening anyway, RBA leadership conveys 
willingness to slow the economy toward the inflation target. 
This stands in contrast to “removing a dose of accommoda-
tion” as Fed Chair Warsh put it. Warsh’s view is confusing 
relative to the committee's depiction (Fed Funds is already 
above the long-run dot). Nevertheless, our global colleagues 
highlight that the rise in medium-term market yields through 
October indeed did not imply any cyclicality: rates were 
priced to reach a potentially higher neutral, and stay there, not 
enough to generate a cyclical decline in growth, and eventual 
unwind in interest rates. 
As discussed here (see Figures 22, 23), global curves have 
also been steeper in markets where our Hawk Dove Scores of 
central banks were turning higher. This is counter to the usual 
relationship where a hawkish shift in policy rhetoric/expecta-
tions flattens the curve, implying most were still not viewed 
as hawkish enough to dampen growing upside risks.
U* is risk tolerance
If the view on capacity is increasingly derived from inflation, 
the policy outlook will be even more sensitive to inflation 
outcomes. This has certainly been true on the way up, which 
we demonstrated in research last week, using the topic shares 
of RBA speeches to derive relative weights in a Taylor Rule. 
The much greater focus on inflation relative to the labour 
market in recent commentary boosts the recommended policy 
rate.
Policy priorities can also shift more radically on the way 
down, when capacity is derived from inflation. The Taylor 
Rule is a natural framework to show this in a context of sup-
ply shocks, since it balances inflation and unemployment. 
Under fixed (U*, r*) parameters, the Balanced Taylor Rule 
recommendation would have jumped in 2H25 on higher infla-
tion, but not have moved much in 2026, as higher inflation 
adds about 130bp, and higher unemployment takes off 90bp.

to a higher U* input, which bumps the rule up 50bp. Inputting 
the RBA’s SoMP forecasts, the unemployment gap is still not 
contributing a lot at the end of 2027. The inflation path does 
all the work in dragging the Taylor Rule lower. 
We forecast the RBA to ease 50bp in 2H27. This is a modest 
amount relative to the economy’s trajectory, respecting the 
fact that after the aborted 2025 easing cycle, lower inflation 
in 2027 would not be initially treated as locked-in. However, 
if inflation falls broadly as expected, risks are skewed to 
more, as it is harder to maintain clearly restrictive policy set-
tings amid consistently weaker activity data, justified only on 
a (shrinking) inflation gap. 
Ultimately, U* assumptions communicate risk tolerance for 
increases in unemployment, and time spent under restrictive 
policy adds to that risk. Passage of time and lower inflation 
then naturally change the risk assessment. Even if the staff 
don’t publicly revise U* lower with realized inflation, the 
board’s actions will still be high-beta to inflation on the way 
down, if lower inflation induces a reassessment of risk. In 
Taylor Rules, this looks the same as lowering U*.

Hawkish Fedspeak, Fed calendar changes
The hawkish Fed rhetoric started with Governor Waller. He 
said just ahead of the CPI release that were there to be a 
0.3%m/m or higher change in the core CPI, then “I believe a 
50 basis point hike in the policy rate should be considered at 
the December meeting. Hiking only 25 basis points would put 
our credibility at risk.” Hours later the core CPI came in at 
0.30% exactly, so it’s fair to say that a 50bp hike will be at 
least discussed at the upcoming meeting. We believe it is still 
more likely that the Committee will go with a 25bp hike. 
Many other FOMC members also spoke. Logan, Schmid, and 
Goolsbee — only Goolsbee votes in 2027 — could be read as 
supporting more hikes past December, while others provided 
less forward guidance. NY Fed President Williams still didn’t 
see “signs of overheating,” and Vice Chair Bowman was 
comparatively dovish, noting there was still “work to do” but 
focusing on uncertainty as to whether AI investment growth 
would remain as rates have increased. 
The Fed also made three changes to its 2027 calendar. The 
first change is that the October 26-27 meeting is now going to 
be the first “Monetary Policy Strategy Meeting” that Warsh 
previewed at his last press conference. No monetary policy 
actions will be taken at this meeting. The choice of October 
perhaps follows from a desire not to make rate changes 
around the time of an election.
The second change was to shift the December 7-8, 2027, 
meeting forward to November 16-17. This gives the FOMC 
another chance to act soon after the October meeting. 
The final change was that the calendar no longer tags which 
meetings will include a Summary of Economic Projections. 
While Warsh hasn’t personally submitted forecasts for the 
SEP, he made no mention of eliminating it at his last press 
conference.  As yet, there has been no further communication 
on this topic, leaving it unclear if the FOMC is eliminating 
the SEP after next month’s meeting. We believe that dropping 
or materially changing the SEP would require wider FOMC 
approval.
Inflation rides again
October delivered the third consecutive upside surprise on 
core CPI, whose 0.30%m/m rise was very similar to the prior 
•
Inflation came in hot again as the CPI delivered anoth-
er firm, broad-based increase 
•
We now expect the the over-year-ago pace on core 
PCE rose back to the recent peak of 3.2%
•
Fedspeak was hawkish to varying degrees immediately 
before and after CPI
•
Next week: a soft retail sales report should deliver flat 
real consumer spending
The inflation news continues to hurt and the Fed speak has 
been hawkish, causing market-implied rates for the December 
Fed meeting to rise 8bp to +27bp and for the June 2027 meet-
ing to rise 39bp to +81bp (Figure 1). The most important 
development was another broad-based rise in the core CPI for 
October. That sets up an estimated 0.29%m/m increase in the 
core PCE deflator, which would leave it up 3.2%oya, equal-
ing the recent peak in May, as well as 3.5% saar over three 
months. This is a brisk pace of inflation in an environment 
where the unemployment rate is around recent lows, price 
signals from business surveys are still near their peak, and 
upstream price inflation in the PPI has been elevated.

It’s also hard to write off the latest run of firm prints as a 
fluke, given the backdrop. Business survey price measures 
have for the most part been gradually rising since the out-
break of the Middle East conflict, with the qualitative details 
pointing to ongoing supply chain difficulties. The NY Fed’s 
Global Supply Chain Pressure index is still near its peak, 
while a negative impulse on goods prices from the end of 
IEEPA tariffs and the tariff refunds may be fading. Tech price 
pass-through into consumer electronics prices is also continu-
ing, though this could moderate in coming months given how 
many firms have already re-priced their product lines.
On the services side, which outside of transportation is less 
sensitive to energy prices and goods pressures, there has also 
been some acceleration, with the three-month pace of super-
core inflation the highest in two and a half years. Still, over 
six months inflation is well within the range of the last couple 
years, highlighting that we probably shouldn’t get too worried 
just yet.

Owner’s equivalent rent has also been doing its part to add to 
inflation over the last couple reports. While we think shelter 
inflation could cool again in coming months, we are keeping 
an eye on new market rents. These take time to pass into 
average rents, but these have notably accelerated this year 
(Figure 3).
The PPI report didn’t deliver the same upside surprise — the 
core was a tenth below our expectations — but upstream 
price pressures for goods are becoming more acute again, 
albeit not as much as in the spring. The core intermediate 
materials PPI sometimes leads changes in final demand goods 
by six to nine months, and that intermediate index has accel-
erated in each of the past three reports. 
Fragile foundations
This week’s existing home sales report showed an already-
weak housing sector coming under further pressure from 
higher interest rates. Sales of previously owned homes fell 
5.5%m/m in October to 3.75mn saar, their lowest level since 
August 2010 and 8.8% below their year-ago pace. The 
decline followed a sharp rise in borrowing costs, with the 
average 30-year fixed mortgage rate climbing to 7.33% in 
October from 6.86% in September. While rising mortgage 
rates might normally be expected to discourage potential sell-
ers from listing their homes, inventories increased in October, 
pushing months’ supply to its highest level since late 2015. 
The combination of improving supply and weak demand 
slowed median price growth to just 0.4%oya, its softest pace 
since February.

Next week’s housing data are likely to remain consistent with 
this downbeat narrative. We expect builder sentiment to edge 
up only slightly from October’s 14-year low, with builders 
continuing to rely heavily on incentives and price reductions 
to support sales. We also see a modest rebound in housing 
starts, largely confined to the volatile multifamily segment 
and accompanied by a further broad-based decline in permits. 
Pending home sales are likely to slip further after reaching a 
record low of their own in September.

We expect the November Empire State manufacturing survey 
to fall to 18.0 from 25.5 in the prior month as the series con-

monthly volatility that could push the index lower in the com-

We expect total retail sales to remain flat in October, follow-
ing a 0.8%m/m print in the prior month, as the series contin-
ues to be impacted by swings in energy prices. While sales at 
gasoline stations jumped 6.4% in the last report, the marginal 
improvement in gas prices is expected to moderate sales in 
the category to a 0.5% rise in October. Sales in the vehicle 
dealers and parts segment are expected to drop by 1.1%, 
informed by the 2.7% decline in unit auto sales, which our 
auto equities team attributed to capital markets and midterm 
volatility along with weaker late-month demand.
Excluding autos and gas, retail sales grew 0.2% in September, 
which we noted as a return to normal after some volatility 
from the change in timing of Amazon’s yearly Prime Day 
event. Overall, as the earlier boost to spending from the tax 
stimulus continues to fade and consumers remain pressured 
by high gas prices, overall consumption could begin to tick 
slightly lower after the strong prints in the recent quarter.
Food services have been a steady boost to headline sales, and 
our Chase card data points to a decent 0.4% rise in October, a 
slightly lower pace than recent months. The control group 
(which excludes food services, autos, gas, and building mate-
rial stores) is expected to rise 0.2%, also informed by our card 
data. The weakness in the September report was mostly from 
declines in general merchandise, sporting goods, and miscel-
laneous retailers, most of which had risen sharply in August. 
Building material sales could rebound after a 0.6% contrac-
tion in September, but remain at a muted overall level, as 
employment in the sector fell over 15k through the month. 14

We look for total import prices to rise 1.1%m/m as global 
energy prices continue to add upward pressure. If realized, the 
over-year-ago pace would mark a cycle high of 9.1%. Much 
of the boost to the energy complex is likely to be attributed to 
persistently higher crude and natural gas prices, coupled with 
slightly lower, yet still elevated, fuel oil.
We project the growth in nonfuel import prices to moderate 
slightly to 0.3%m/m from 0.7% in the prior month, leaving 
the over-year-ago pace stable at 6.0%. Computer prices 
remain on an upward trajectory, rising 2.4% in September, 
while outside of this, nonfuel prices rose 0.4%. After increas-
ing by close to 1% cumulatively over the last two months, the 
growth in consumer prices could moderate in October, espe-
cially as import inflation begins to reflect the earlier apprecia-
tion in the dollar. However, as we noted earlier, this category 
is also buffeted by adjustments in pre-tariff prices in response 
to changes in trade policies.

We look for total Industrial Production in October to rise 
0.1%m/m after a strong 0.7% rise in the prior month. Motor 
vehicle production fell 3.0% in September and is down 
15.1%ar over the last three months, although the production 
schedules for October point to some recovery in this segment. 
Strong durable goods orders for aircraft are likely to keep out-
put at solid levels into the end of the year, after a soft 0.5% 
rise in the last report. High-tech production is also likely to 
remain a strong contributor to manufacturing IP as the seg-
ment is boosted by the ongoing surge in data center buildouts.
Outside of autos, we expect manufacturing output to rise 
0.1%m/m as average weekly hours for production and non-
supervisory workers moved broadly sideways through the 
month. The national manufacturing surveys sent mixed sig-
nals in October, with the ISM production index rising from 
56.7 to 59.0 while the PMI measure of output edged down 
from 56.2 to 54.5.
The 3.5% jump in utilities in September could begin to wane 
in the coming report, along with some contraction in mining 
output, weighing on headline IP for October. The strong Sep-
tember report also saw a 1.0% rise in chemicals, 1.9% 
increase in printing, 1.3% growth in electrical equipment, and 
a 1.6% uptick in primary metals.

We look for the NAHB Housing Market Index to edge up to 
30 in November after slumping to a 14-year low of 29 in the 
prior month. Rising mortgage rates and affordability concerns 
are weighing on sales activity. The present sales component 
of the survey has been falling since the end of last year and 
dropped a further 2 points in October. The index for prospec-
tive buyer traffic fell 5 points in October after moving side-
ways in a narrow range since the beginning of the year. Senti-
ment is unlikely to improve materially in the coming months, 
and builders continue to rely on incentives and price cuts to 
prop up sales. The press release for October also flagged hir-
ing challenges from increased immigration enforcement

We expect overall housing starts to rise 2.0%m/m in October 
to 1.245mn from 1.221mn in September. Much of this reflects 
the volatile multifamily segment, which could rebound after 
contracting over 25% cumulatively in the last two reports. 
Single-family starts fell to a 12-month low in September, as 
the series declined by 5.2% on the month. We also look for a 
slight 1.0%m/m decline in housing permits to 1.385mn from 
1.398mn in September, led by ongoing weakness in the sin-
gle-family category.
As builders increasingly rely on price cuts and incentives to 
work through existing inventories, new residential construc-
tion is unlikely to rebound in the coming months. This is 
reflected in homebuilder sentiment, with the October NAHB 
HMI falling to the lowest since June 2021. Single-family per-
mits have fallen 5.4% since August.

We expect the November Philadelphia Fed manufacturing 
survey to remain steady at 20.0 from 20.9 in October. The 
series recently reached a cycle high of 47.4 in August, before 
falling to 20.9 in the last report, with the ISM-adjusted com-
posite now back to levels seen during the middle of the year. 
Many of the details of the survey reflect a similar trend, as 
new orders went from 37.0 in July to 4.3 in October and ship-
ments fell from 33.7 to 15.3.
Some of this moderation was also reflected in delivery times, 
which erased much of the spike in September, although sup-
ply chain challenges have pushed inventories to their lowest 
in close to three years. The price indexes mirrored their 
Empire counterparts in October, with prices paid inching 
down to 43.2 from 48.6, while prices received surged to 40.1 
from 31.3.

1. Payroll survey week
Thousands, sa
New claims (wr.)
Continuing claims
We expect initial jobless claims to inch down to 195k in the 
week ending November 14 from 200k in the first week of 
November. While the last report saw claims rise to the highest 
level in a month, the series continues to maintain a steadily 
expanding gap to prior years’ levels. Residual seasonality is 
likely to push the series lower into the end of the year.
Continuing claims have been volatile within a narrow range 
in recent weeks, falling 20k in the last week of October, fol-
lowing a 22k rise in the prior week. The over-year-ago pace 
of decline in the four-week average of continuing claims con-
tinues to increase, with the series now down 12.5%oya, point-
ing to the unemployment rate remaining in the low 4% range.

and core services rising 0.34% (0.40% ex rent and OER). 
Because of last year’s government shutdown, there is no offi-
cial 12-month increase in prices available. The rise in the 
headline CPI for October was also supported by a 0.5%m/m 
increase in food prices, the largest gain since April. Food at 
home rose 0.4%, with gains across every major category 
except cereals/bakery and beverages. 
Turning back to the core, overall goods prices were held 
down by a 1.2%m/m decline in used vehicle prices, the larg-
est drop since January. The 0.45%m/m increase in core goods 
ex new and used vehicles was the second largest rise in the 
post-COVID period. For core services, the firm 0.34% 
increase was similar to the prior couple months. While rent 
(0.20%) moderated there was another high-side increase in 
OER (0.32%), which was also less geographically concentrat-
ed than the prior month’s 0.35% gain. Outside of rent and 
OER, notable sources of upside pressure in services included 
transportation services (0.8%), which included a 0.4% rise in 
airfare, mild in relation to recent months, and a 0.7% increase 
in motor vehicle insurance.

Existing home sales (Nov 12)
Existing home sales fell 5.5%m/m in October to 3.75mn saar, 
their lowest level since August 2010. The decline left sales 
down 8.8% from a year ago, the largest year-ago decline in 
three years. On the supply side, the number of existing homes 
for sale increased 1.3% on the month and 4.6% from a year 
ago, reaching 1.59mn units. With demand weakening, 
months’ supply rose to 5.1 from 4.7 in September and 4.4 a 
year earlier, its highest level since late 2015. Affordability 
deteriorated further in October, with the NAR’s affordability 
index falling to its lowest level in over a year.

PPI (Nov 13)
Headline final demand PPI rose 0.3%m/m (and a new cycle 
high 6.5%oya), and core PPI also rose 0.3%. Energy was flat 
and food jumped 1.3%. The core had increased 0.8% the prior 
month, so October was a return to somewhat more mild read-
ings. That was driven by services, where trade services 
(wholesale and retail margins) fell 0.2% after a near-record 
2.1% gain the prior month, and services ex trade and trans-
portation rose 0.2%. News was less favorable in transporta-
tion and warehousing services, which had another large 2.0% 
increase on the back of higher fuel costs. While core services 
moderated on the month, core goods inflation picked up. The 
core goods PPI rose 0.5%m/m, the most since May, with 
stronger inflation on goods bound for government or export.

The second GDP release still lacked the expenditure break-
down, but through 1H26 the economy was supported by 
domestic demand, whose contribution to growth continues to 
run above-pre-pandemic-average on an ex-Ireland basis (Fig-
ure 2). Government consumption strengthened, while private 
consumption proved remarkably resilient, growing at around 
last year’s 1%ar pace (likely reflecting the smaller-than-ex-
pected hit to real disposable income, alongside a boost to 
durable-goods purchases as consumers switched towards 
hybrids and EVs) — The latter stands in sharp contrast to our 
expectations at the onset of the conflict, when we had expect-
ed consumption to contract. These two components have 
more than offset contracting investment, as higher rates and 
the confidence shock weighed particularly on housing invest-
ment. Net exports also rebounded in 1H, with anecdotal evi-
dence suggesting some support from inventory dynamics and 
an initial competitiveness advantage relative to peers follow-
ing the conflict.

Employment stalled in 3Q
Based on the country releases available so far, our sense is 
that, alongside continued support from government demand, 
private consumption remained broadly resilient in 3Q, and we 
retain our forecast for a 1%ar increase. But that resilience still 
looks fragile. The labour market continues to tell a less reas-
suring story, with employment essentially flatlining in 3Q, as 
data released today confirmed our tracking (Figure 3). This 
adds to the downside risks around our near-term outlook, 
•
Euro area GDP estimate unrevised at 0.8% ar in 3Q26 
ex. Ireland
•
That left growth close to potential, but employment 
growth stalled
•
Energy prices also remained sticky this week after 
moving lower last week
•
Growth likely to pick up if energy prices fall; ECB still 
on track to deliver (at least) two more hikes
The 3Q26 stall in Euro area employment growth is a reminder 
of weaker parts in the Euro area data flow and that continued 
growth resilience requires energy prices to move lower. These 
looked stickier again this week: gas prices futures moved 
back above the ECB staff’s adverse scenario and approached 
it for oil. This also pushed rate expectations higher again, 
while upcoming elections and recent protests pose risks. At 
the same time, protests were not widespread and may be los-
ing momentum, and our simple driver-based GDP model now 
explains almost all of the resilience since early last year, 
largely due to upward revisions to trade-partner growth. The 
latter still leaves GDP outturns on the expenditure side having 
mostly been driven by domestic demand over the past two 
years. Overall, there are cross-current and weak patches, but 
as long as energy prices eventually move lower there remains 
a case for expecting a period of above-potential growth. 
3Q growth resilience confirmed
Eurostat’s second estimate for 3Q26 GDP confirmed the pre-
liminary estimate that showed the Euro area economy 
expanded at a 1.7% annualised rate. The strong headline 
number was partly driven by Ireland, where the data are vola-
tile and distorted by the activity of multinationals. Ex-Ireland, 
however, the message remains one of resilience, with growth 
at 0.8%ar. Overall, growth has averaged 1.1%ar this year, 
broadly in line with our estimate of potential and last year’s 
pace, despite heightened uncertainty, the renewed energy 
shock and somewhat tighter financial conditions. The October 
PMI is consistent with continued resilience into 4Q, pointing 
to a pickup in growth to around 1.25%ar, as strong activity in 
countries such as Germany and Spain largely offsets more 
localised weakness in France and Italy (Figure 1; for more, 
see here). From 1Q27, we continue to expect growth to pick 
up to 1.5%ar as in our baseline the energy shock starts to fade 
— though risks remain skewed to the downside.

As we discussed recently, the Euro area continues to operate 
with a two-speed labour market: employment has remained 
relatively firm in the periphery but has been contracting in the 
core, where Germany remains the main drag, while French 
labour demand has also softened. Until recently, stronger job 
creation in the periphery was sufficient to offset this weak-
ness, but that cushion is narrowing as hiring momentum there 
normalises. Looking ahead, we expect employment growth to 
recover modestly to around 0.3%ar in 4Q26 and strengthen to 
around 0.5%ar from 1Q27 as activity firms. The latest PMI 
employment indicators support the direction of a near-term 
bounce, but do not yet establish that a durable recovery is 
underway. Until it does, weak labour demand remains an 
important downside risk to the consumer recovery we expect 
from next quarter, both by weighing on labour-income growth 
and, potentially, by weakening confidence and encouraging 
precautionary saving.
A reduced level of mystery in the PSR
Back in September, we published an update on our PSR 
(Potential, Shocks and Residual) growth model. This was 
broadly consistent with a resilient economy, as the energy 
shock was partly offset by other growth drivers that were still 
improving (Figure 4 and Table 1). The PSR nevertheless 
struggled to fully explain the actual level of growth and there-
fore had a sizeable residual for 2024, 2025 and parts of 2026. 
The PSR was also not fully aligned with our forecast for 
2027. Updating the model with the latest data still shows a 
gap in 2024, but it now much better explains growth in 2025 
and so far in 2026. The main change is the upward revisions 
to the trade-weighted GDP growth have come through, which 
as strengthened the contribution from the “global cycle” vari-
able. This strengthens the case for growth to hold up, 
although we incorporate in the PSR an assumed moderation 
in energy prices, which has yet to fully materialize.

France: Le Pen remains ahead in the polls 
The French presidential election campaign is still in its early 
stages. Polls are hard to assess five months ahead of the elec-
tion, but the gap between Le Pen and the other candidates is 
statistically large, making a Le Pen victory a base case sce-
nario at this stage. Recent polls have been pointing to clear 
dynamics. Support for Glucksmann (centre-left), ex. PM Attal 
(centre-right) and ex. PM Philippe (right wing) has declined 
in recent weeks. Meanwhile, support for Mélenchon (far-left) 
has gained momentum. Stopping the clock today would sug-
gest that Mélenchon could be joining Le Pen for the second 
round of the election. Polls since the summer have on average 
been suggesting that Le Pen would win with 67.4% of the 
votes against Melenchon. This statistically gives Le Pen a 
very strong chance to win the election. A key question is 
whether Le Pen will be able to run for the presidency. The 
common wisdom is that she can, but the story is not that sim-
ple. The Court of Cassation still needs to rule regarding Le 
Pen's embezzlement conviction. Another key question is 
whether Le Pen would also need to get a majority in the lower 
house. A lower house election after the presidential election is 
likely but hard to call at this stage.

We expect the Euro area final print for October to come in 
line with the flash release.  The flash data showed that Euro 
area headline inflation increased further, a move skewed 
towards energy price inflation. Food price inflation also 
increased slightly, but remains low considering significant 
price pressures in recent months (higher energy prices and

broadly stable: a 0.04%-pt increase to 2.54%oya. The data 
overall have been pointing to a significant pass-through from 
market energy prices to headline inflation in recent months, 
broadly in line with our models. However, the pass-through 
of higher energy prices to core prices so far has been limited.

J P M O R G A N
sector. Higher raw material and labor costs have squeezed 
profit margins, leaving little room for holding prices—let 
alone cutting them. 
Make haste slowly
In the monetary policy section of the BoJ’s summary of opin-
ions, there was a major change to the opening comment 
which could be seen as reflecting the leadership members’ 
view. Previously, the section typically set out a gradual rate-
hike approach. This time, however, that was replaced with a 
framework for assessing financial conditions, stating that 
“With respect to financial conditions, it is appropriate to 
assess them using three measures: the real interest rate gap, 
corporate finance and the bank lending channel, and asset 
prices.” This indicates that, as the BoJ continues to raise 
rates, it intends to use the degree of tightening in financial 
conditions as a guide to the pace and terminal level of rate 
hikes. Another passage that appears to signal the BoJ’s inten-
tions stated that “the rise in stock prices has been mainly led 
by the global expansion in AI-related demand, and it is thus 
necessary to bear in mind that factors other than monetary 
policy could also significantly change the situation in these 
prices.” We interpret this as a warning to some market partici-
pants who believe that the recent stock-market correction 
could make it difficult for the BoJ to raise rates. In other 
words, while the BoJ will assess financial conditions using 
the three-part framework set out at the beginning of the sec-
tion, it appears likely to give less weight to movements in 
asset prices. 
Comments on the economy did not reveal any notable con-
cerns. However, questions were raised about the durability of 
AI-related demand, and private consumption was described as 
“resilient against the background of an improvement in the 
employment and income situation, but it is likely that it does 
not have the momentum to lead the overall economy.” Yet, in 
comments on how rate hikes affect the economy, one view 
was that “the underlying trend in consumption should be 
assessed, given structural factors stemming from the declin-
ing and aging population.” If these factors are recognized, the 
threshold for  the BoJ judging consumption to be resilient 
should not be particularly high. 
The comments from the government side this time did not 
appear to be an attempt to pressure the BoJ’s decision-mak-
ing. This may have been because the meeting did not result in 
a rate hike, but overt pressure from the Cabinet Office has 
receded since the September meeting. 
•
BoJ’s opinions signaled continued rate hikes toward 
neutral, likely at a roughly quarterly pace
•
Business sentiment and services consumption remain 
strong, while spending by low- and middle-income 
households continues to lag
•
The planned food consumption tax holiday prompts 
mixed pricing strategy, but higher costs are likely to 
limit price cuts across food services sector
•
Next week: Soft 3Q GDP growth, October trade and 
CPI
The BoJ released the main opinions from its October meeting, 
again signaling its intention to continue raising interest rates 
by “making haste slowly” toward what it considers to be a 
neutral level, while taking financial conditions into account. 
Although the neutral rate remains uncertain, many members 
appear to believe that, given the current accommodative 
financial conditions, the policy rate still has further to go 
from its current level of 1.25%. This is consistent with our 
expectation that the BoJ will continue to raise rates roughly 
once a quarter through the middle of next year. 
Indeed, this week’s data suggest that easing inflationary pres-
sures through BoJ rate hikes could help improve the balance 
of the economy. November’s large manufacturers’ sentiment 
marked a surprisingly strong gain, supported by robust AI-re-
lated investment. Sentiment among large non-manufacturers 
also remained elevated, reflecting strong services consump-
tion. Despite a significant rise in input costs since the spring, 
large firms’ sentiment has been notably stronger than that of 
SMEs, underpinned by their strong pricing power. These 
imbalances are also evident in consumption. The BoJ’s Sep-
tember CAI again showed that, while overall consumption 
remains resilient, non-durable goods consumption continues 
to trend downward, while services consumption remains 
strong. We believe that this persistent decline in non-durable 
goods consumption reflects not only demographic changes 
but also the squeeze on real incomes caused by inflation, 
which is particularly pronounced among low- and middle-in-
come households. 
Last week, the bill for the consumption tax cut on food easily 
sailed through the Lower House. With PM Takaichi’s “earnest 
wish” now all but certain to begin in April 2027, firms’ 
responses have diverged. Some food manufacturers are plan-
ning price hikes ahead of the tax cut. By contrast, some major 
restaurant operators have announced price cuts, aiming to 
prevent customers from drifting away even as dining out 
remains excluded from the tax cut. However, we do not 
expect price cuts to be widespread across the food services

Sep CAI edges up on holiday-related 
demand
The BoJ’s Consumption Activity Index (CAI, travel-balance 
adjusted) edged up 0.2%m/m sa in September, following a 
0.9% decline in August. The increase was solid given head-
winds from unfavorable weather since August and a contin-
ued payback from the earlier lift in durable goods consump-
tion. In September, holiday-related demand over the extended 
holiday period likely provided some support to non-durables 
and services. Although goods spending declined in 3Q26, ser-
vices spending continued to rise at a 4.0%q/q saar pace, sug-
gesting that the underlying spending trend has not materially 
weakened　(Figure 1). 
We maintain our 3Q26 GDP tracking estimate at 0.5%q/q 
saar, with GDP-based real private consumption increasing 
0.2%. Although the CAI declined 2.4%q/q saar after a strong 
2Q, we expect GDP consumption to post a firmer result than 
the CAI suggests, reflecting the fading of policy-related dis-
tortions in the GDP data. If realized, our forecast would point 
to a near-flat outcome in two consecutive quarters. Looking 
ahead, however, we expect consumption to strengthen in 4Q 
as the effects of the durable-goods payback and adverse 
weather fade, with solid wage growth providing further sup-
port.

The September breakdown shows the durable-goods index 
fell 1.4%m/m sa, as payback in auto and air conditioner sales 
continued　(Figure 2). However, the pace of decline is slow-
ing, pointing to a potential normalization in the coming 
months. The non-durable goods index rose 0.4%m/m sa, pos-
sibly reflecting stronger apparel and general merchandise 
demand over the extended holiday period, as suggested by the 
retail sales report. The boost from holiday demand was also 
evident in services spending: the services index rose 0.3%m/
m sa, likely supported by demand for dining out and travel.
November Tankan signals broad-based 
strength
The November Reuters Tankan survey was surprisingly 
strong, suggesting broad-based improvement in business 
activity across sectors. Sentiment among large manufacturers 
rose 5 points to 27, its highest level since August 2021 (Fig-
ure 3). This is consistent with our view that the uptrend in 
manufacturing production is continuing, supported by AI-re-
lated demand that is broadening to machinery and materials. 
Sentiment among large non-manufacturers jumped 12 points 
to 35, its highest since June 2018. The three-month outlook 
also improved from October in both sectors: manufacturing 
rose 5pts to 28, while non-manufacturing increased 8pts to 
29.

We expect the first estimate of 3Q26 real GDP to show a clear 
slowdown in growth, reflecting temporary factors such as 
adverse weather and natural disasters. Personal consumption 
is expected to have grown only modestly, weighed down by 
the reversal of the sharp 2Q boost to durable goods driven in 
part by policy factors, as well as by adverse weather. Business 
investment is likely to have recovered from two consecutive 
quarters of contraction, supported by an easing of supply con-
straints related to the Middle East conflict and a rebound from 
the sharp 2Q decline in intellectual property investment. 
Exports growth is expected to have slowed, as weak services 
exports offset solid goods exports. Imports, by contrast, are 
expected to have rebounded sharply, making net exports a 
significant drag on growth. Inventory activity is also likely to 
have shifted from a drawdown to an accumulation, turning 
inventories’ contribution to growth positive.

The household-activity index was the main drag in October, 
likely reflecting SMEs’ concerns about the continued acceler-
ation in inflation. Many respondents expressed concern that 
higher consumer prices could weigh on consumption, while 
rising input costs could squeeze profit margins. Sector-level 
results were mixed. Within household activity, the retail index 
fell 2.1pts to 43.4, its lowest level since June. Conversely, 
comments from the food and services sectors pointed to con-
sumer resilience. The food-sector index rose 2.0pts to 48.7, 
its highest level since September 2024, likely supported by 
lower rice prices and solid inbound tourism. The services-sec-
tor index declined by just 0.7pts, following a cumulative 7.7pt 
increase from May through September.
The business activity-related index increased, and multiple 
respondents reported sustained strength in orders and greater 
appetite for capex. However, some noted that higher input 
costs were weighing on margins, particularly in some manu-
facturing sectors including food products and machinery. The 
business-activity outlook index fell, driven by non-manufac-
turing, with respondents citing intensified cost pressures, par-
ticularly in construction. Rising cyberattack incidents also 
likely weighed on sentiment in information services.

Goods PPI firmed further in October, reflecting the continu-
ing effects of the energy price shock. Import prices rose 
2.3%m/m, driven by further increases in oil and tech prices. 
Higher input costs are feeding through to domestic producer 
prices: petroleum product prices rose 15.9%oya, while chemi-
cal prices increased 15.5%oya. Although our commodity ana-
lysts expect crude oil prices to decline toward year-end, 
petroleum product prices—particularly naphtha—are likely to 
remain firm, reflecting the additional costs of alternative 
sourcing. The persistence of these producer-price pressures 
raises the risk of further upward pressure on consumer infla-
tion.

Bennett Parrish (1-212) 622-9003
bennett.parrish@jpmchase.com

Canada

J P M O R G A N
2.50%. The case for holding rested on the concentration of 
inflation in energy and energy-sensitive services, core infla-
tion near 2%, continued economic slack, and the expected 
drag from new US tariffs. The case for hiking focused instead 
on building business costs and the likelihood that firms would 
eventually pass more of those increases on to customers.
The meeting minutes reinforced that the Bank acted before 
broad pass-through had become visible in the inflation data. 
Members agreed that monetary policy cannot lower global 
energy prices but can reduce the risk that higher energy costs 
produce generalized and persistent inflation. Although Gov-
erning Council members held different views on the likely 
speed and extent of pass-through, they concluded that higher 
energy and imported input costs would eventually reach con-
sumer prices if the shock persisted. That assessment was sup-
ported by the Bank’s quarterly surveys, in which firms report-
ed plans to pass through a greater share of their recent cost 
increases over the coming year.
A more resilient growth outlook also made it easier for the 
BoC to act. The Governing Council judged that stronger con-
sumption, exports, and business investment would gradually 
absorb the remaining economic slack, leaving less downward 
pressure on prices to offset rising costs. Members neverthe-
less acknowledged continued labor market softness and 
uncertainty surrounding Canada-US trade. The discussion 
broadly supports our view that the October hike was a precau-
tionary move rather than the start of an automatic sequence of 
increases. Further tightening remains possible, but the Bank 
intends to proceed carefully and with the benefit of incoming 
information.
Consumer spending resilient in 3Q
We will also receive September retail sales next week. We 
expect nominal sales to rise 0.7%m/m, broadly in line with 
Statistics Canada’s preliminary estimate. However, a sharp 
increase in goods prices should absorb most of that nominal 
gain, leaving real retail sales roughly unchanged.
Even with a flat September reading, consumers appear to 
have remained resilient in 3Q. We estimate that total real con-
sumer spending increased at a still-solid 2.6% annualized 
pace, supported by income and wealth gains despite elevated 
trade uncertainty and softer labor market conditions. 
We expect consumer spending growth to slow below 2% 
annualized in 4Q. Higher energy prices are eroding household 
purchasing power, while the unemployment rate has risen to 
6.7% and employment has declined for three consecutive 
months. Housing activity has also remained subdued, and 
higher borrowing costs should weigh increasingly on interest-
sensitive spending. 
•
Summary of Deliberations confirmed that the October 
BoC hike was aimed at preventing cost pass-through
•
A firm October core CPI reading would materially 
raise the risk of another 25bp hike in December
•
Consumer spending remained solid in 3Q but slowed 
as higher energy costs weighed on purchasing power
Next week’s October CPI report will be the most important 
input into the Bank of Canada’s December decision. We esti-
mate that the exclusionary core CPI rose 0.2%m/m, sa in 
October, nudging the year-ago rate up a tenth to 2.0%. Head-
line CPI likely increased 0.3%m/m, sa, lifting the year-ago 
rate to 3.2% from 3.1%. Gasoline prices fell 2.4% on the 
month, but an unfavorable base effect should more than offset 
that decline in the year-ago comparison.
A firm core CPI reading would support the BoC’s concern 
that persistently elevated energy and imported input costs are 
beginning to pass through to other goods and services. That 
could be enough to tip the Governing Council toward another 
25bp increase in December. For now, however, we expect 
underlying inflation to remain sufficiently contained to allow 
the Bank to pause and assess the effects of its October hike 
before tightening again (Figure 1).

another move at the next meeting.

Inflation spikes in October to 3.75%
We had argued that the recent benign momentum in inflation 
would be short-lived as the economy firmed and energy and 
food commodity prices put upward pressure on inflation. 
Now, the recent weakness in the Mexican peso is adding 
renewed pressures. Indeed, the October inflation print showed 
a surprising deterioration, as pressures on both core and non-
core indexes pushed headline inflation higher. In the event, 
inflation rose to 3.75%oya from 3.45% in September, above 
consensus’ 3.65%, and closer to our 3.72% estimate. Core 
inflation increased to 3.80% on renewed services pressures 
(Figure 2). Meanwhile, the more volatile non-core component 
mean-reverted significantly, jumping to 3.59% from 2.45%.

Minutes preview: Seeking clarifications
•
October inflation increased to 3.75%oya; we now see 
year-end a tenth higher to 3.8%
•
September’s industrial production fall revives head-
winds 
•
Retail sales expected to moderate as well at the end of 
3Q  
Following Banxico’s surprise decision to cut the policy rate to 
6.25%, next week’s minutes will be closely watched to gauge 
the renewed dovishness of the Board. However, the minutes 
are unlikely to provide any major signals on future decisions. 
While we believe the decision to cut was risky in the context 
of Fed hikes and weak inflation anchoring, the cautious tone 
that members are likely to reinforce after implementing one 
last cut this year should appease market concerns for the time 
being — provided an emphasis on vigilance and prudence is 
underscored. This cautious tone should be present given that 
inflationary risks stemming from energy and food prices, and 
a weaker peso are now appearing. In fact, after this week's 
October CPI release evidenced some pass-through concerns, 
we updated our 2026 CPI forecast a tenth higher to 3.8%. 
Also next week, September retail sales should show a modest 
decline (-0.3%samr), consistent with this week’s weaker-
than-anticipated industrial production print (-1.4%samr). 
Both add some downside risks to our 2% full-year growth 
forecast. At the margin, this has been the only factor playing 
in favor of the dovish board.
Banxico minutes: No Easter eggs  
The relevance of analyzing the discussions that lead to mone-
tary policy decisions (i.e. the minutes) had an important 
upgrade last month after the September discussion provided a 
notable detour from the statement — particularly when 
deconstructing the comments from the dovish majority. Rath-
er than elaborating on the statement guidance, the minutes 
appeared to be a companion to the communiqué, reinforcing a 
message that was not clear enough in its initial guidance. This 
time around, we don’t believe the minutes to the November 
decision will work in the same way.  
After cutting 25bps to 6.25% in a divided decision, we 
believe the two dissenters will elaborate on the caution war-
ranted amid Fed hikes, the lack of expectations anchoring, 
and the need to remain vigilant ahead of mounting inflation-
ary risks and global policy tightening. Yet, we are particularly 
interested in having answers to some of these questions: Why 
cut when the door was closed in May and inflation is starting 
to climb higher and long-term inflation expectations have not

Seasonally adjusted data further reinforce the deterioration in 
inflation dynamics. Headline inflation rose 0.50%samr, while 
core increased 0.38%. Renewed pressure within services was 
evident as it rose 0.45%, its highest seasonally adjusted 
monthly increase since July 2024. Core goods rose a more 
sanguine 0.29%. This left headline inflation running at 
3.0%3m/3m saar and core at 3.3%. 
In sum, the upside inflation pressures we had previously cau-
tioned about are starting to materialize, leading us to revise 
our 2026 headline inflation forecast one-tenth higher to 3.8%, 
while adding upside risks to our 2026 core forecast and to our 
forecasts for 2027. 
September IP fuels downside risks to 
growth
Since the release of the 3Q26 flash GDP print (3.7%saar) and 
the September survey data, we have signaled that end-of-
quarter data would be significantly cooler than those observed 
in the April-August span. Indeed, industrial production mod-
erated from 3.5%oya  in August, to 1.3% in September. This 
left year-to-date growth at 1.1%. In monthly terms, IP fell 
1.4%samr, significantly worse than our -0.1% estimate. Ser-
vices data out next week will be important to fine tune our 
view, but downside risks to this year are now evident.
Worth noting, the details embedded in this week’s release 
showed that the weakness was not widespread. While the 
heftiest sectors, namely construction and manufacturing, fell 
2.0% samr and 1.7%, respectively, mining and utilities 
increased (Figure 3). Mining rose 0.4% following a very 
weak start to the last quarter. Meanwhile, utilities increased 
1.4%, marking its fourth consecutive gain.

Better consumption, still weak momentum
Together with stagnant services, declining industrial produc-
tion and little sustained investment growth, the broader slow-
down story remains intact. As a result, the downside risks to 
our flat 3Q GDP baseline are still very much alive.
That said, the September releases were a bit better than antici-
pated, in general. Services output fell 0.1% m/m sa in Sep-
tember, after a flat reading in August. Services to families 
were flat on the month but remained relatively strong over the 
quarter, although recent readings suggest some cooling. Retail 
sales, in turn, provided a more favorable reading. Core and 
broad sales both rose 0.2% m/m sa, compared with our fore-
casts of -0.6% and -0.2%, respectively. 
The gains therefore led to another increase in our consump-
tion activity index for the month, though household spending 
and investment growth were still overall weak last quarter 
(Figure 2).

CPI: Encouraging details, persistent risks
October IPCA rose 0.14% m/m, below our 0.23% forecast 
and the 0.25% consensus. Lower-than-expected gasoline pric-
es explained around 5bp of the 9bp miss relative to our fore-
cast, but the more relevant news was the broader relief in 
underlying inflation. Underlying services increased 0.26% m/
m, with the downside surprise spread across items. Mean core 
inflation and industrial goods also came in below our esti-
mates, making the composition more encouraging than a 
•
The proposed fiscal package signals a response to bud-
get pressures but delivers limited structural progress
•
This reinforces our view of a piecemeal strategy aimed 
at gradual improvements in the primary balance
•
October CPI was soft, while recent activity data were 
somewhat firmer
•
We retain our call for the Selic to remain at 13.75%, 
but risks remain skewed to the upside
Our base case is that fiscal policy will continue to follow a 
piecemeal strategy, delivering only very gradual near-term 
improvements in the primary balance, largely via higher tax-
es. In our assessment, this week’s proposed fiscal package is 
consistent with that view: it sends a political signal that bud-
get pressures are being addressed, but its contribution to a 
durable adjustment remains uncertain.
The package appears designed to respond to budget con-
straints while protecting spending priorities. The fiscal 
impact, however, is less certain. Much of the estimated 
R$45–54bn improvement in the 2027 primary balance rests 
on discretionary spending restraint and restrictions on tax 
credits, while key benefit formulas remain intact. With pre-
election measures creating an estimated R$40bn in unbudget-
ed pressures, a meaningful portion of the budgetary correc-
tion may simply offset earlier commitments rather than 
improve the underlying fiscal position.
A further constitutional package is reportedly still subject to 
presidential approval, leaving the scope and durability of the 
broader response unsettled. At this stage, we do not yet envi-
sion durable changes in the fiscal stance and continue to fore-
cast a primary result next year that materially undershoots the 
government’s target of 0.5% of GDP, particularly if growth 
continues to decelerate.
To the extent that the fiscal approach affects the exchange rate 
and inflation expectations, it will shape the BCB’s ability to 
maintain its current stance. The minutes of the last monetary-
policy decision suggest a high bar for rate hikes, with existing 
restraint expected to slow demand and limit second-round 
effects from supply shocks. This week’s data were less 
adverse than expected, but the ongoing depreciation of the 
exchange rate and the likely continued deterioration of infla-
tion expectations leave upside risks to our baseline of a con-
stant Selic through end-2027 (Figure 1).

Seasonal and regulated price pressures 
expected to ease in November
Our high-frequency inflation tracker indicates that headline 
CPI is running at 1.8% m/m in the first two weeks of Novem-
ber, including the lagged impact of the “cold zones” regime 
change, which we estimate will add 0.2 percentage points this 
month. We will continue to monitor incoming high-frequency 
data and adjust our monthly forecast as needed.
Fuel prices have remained relatively contained, rising 2.4% 
m/m and 30.2% year to date, despite the strong increase in 
international oil prices in October and year to date. Following 
the recent decline in Brent, however, we do not expect local 
fuel prices to fall, as YPF’s “buffer mechanism” is designed 
to smooth short-term volatility. The government has also once 
again deferred the remaining accumulated fuel-tax increases, 
this time until December 1.
Against this backdrop, we expect headline inflation to aver-
age 1.9% m/m over the final two months of the year, consis-
tent with a December 2026 rate of 31% y/y. We expect 
monthly inflation to average 1.8% in 1Q27, remaining sticky 
amid El Niño-related pressures and the frontloading of regu-
lated price increases for the rest of the year. In 2H27, we 
expect monthly inflation to average 1.9%, reflecting tradable 
CPI pressures associated with election-driven volatility (Fig-
ure 2). This path is consistent with a year-end inflation fore-
cast of 23% y/y and limited average REER depreciation of 
5%. Risks are skewed to the upside if FX-related pressures 
prove stronger than expected amid rising domestic and global 
uncertainty.

Headline inflation rose 2.2% m/m in October, above both our 
expectation and BCRA survey consensus of 1.9% and 1.8%, 
respectively. The monthly rate accelerated from September 
and the prior three-month average of 1.9% m/m. On an annu-
al basis, however, headline inflation edged down to 33.1% y/
y. Cumulative inflation reached 26.3% year to date, compared 
with 24.8% over the same period in 2025.
The acceleration was driven mainly by seasonal and regulated 
prices, which rose 5.5% m/m and 2.9% m/m, respectively, 
and together added 1.1 percentage points to the headline 
print, up from 0.8 percentage points the prior month. Seasonal 
inflation reflected a 10% m/m increase in fresh vegetable 
prices, following a 9.7% rise in September. Regulated infla-
tion was driven by higher gas tariffs, revisions to subsidized 
consumption thresholds, and changes to the “cold zones” 
regime, which provides climate-based subsidies. We estimate 
that the latter added 0.3 percentage points to headline infla-
tion. Other regulated prices rose more modestly: healthcare 
increased 1.7% m/m and education 1.3% m/m. Year to date, 
regulated prices, seasonal items, and meat have contributed 
12.1 percentage points to headline inflation—almost half of 
the cumulative increase (Figure 1).

Activity strengthens in September, October 
early data mixed
Following the weak July activity print at the start of the quar-
ter and the mild uptick in August, sectoral indicators point to 
a stronger reading in September. On the supply side, the latest 
data published by INDEC support this view: manufacturing 
rose 0.7% m/m sa, while construction jumped 7.8% m/m sa. 
This may partly reflect the start of the construction phase of 
the national government’s highway privatization program.
Demand-side indicators present a more mixed picture. Auto 
sales fell 2.0% m/m sa in September, following a strong 5.0% 
increase in August. The Universidad Torcuato Di Tella’s con-
sumer confidence index also declined, by 5.9% m/m nsa. By 
contrast, total tax revenues reversed its August decline, rising 
1.5% m/m sa, supported in part by a rebound in VAT-DGI 
collection (+2.7% m/m sa).

However, the early-month activity indicators for October 
released so far point to a continuation of the sawtooth pattern 
seen in previous months. Cement sales softened in October, 
falling 7.6% m/m sa after rising 9.0% m/m sa in September, 
partly reflecting higher precipitation during the month. 
Sequentially, total tax revenues were broadly flat (+0.4% m/m 
sa), while consumption-related VAT (VAT-DGI) revenues 
edged down 0.1% m/m sa.
Meanwhile, auto production was flat in October, marking its 
third consecutive month without a contraction—the first such 
streak since January 2025. Domestic car sales recovered from 
September’s decline, rising 4.0% m/m sa in October. Senti-
ment indicators were supportive as well: consumer and gov-
ernment confidence indexes increased 0.8% and 0.9% m/m 
nsa, respectively.
Even so, the sharp contraction in activity in July, together 
with the modest recovery in August, continues to weigh on 
the quarterly outturn. Recent developments therefore intro-
duce downside risk to our 1.5% y/y full-year 2026 GDP fore-
cast. At the same time, the outlook for strong export-volume 
growth continues to support our 2.5% y/y GDP projection for 
2027.

Meanwhile, revenues have yet to provide meaningful support 
to the consolidation effort. Real gross tax collection increased 
just 0.2%oya in August-September and remains down 
0.3%oya year-to-date. VAT remains the main source of posi-
tive real growth, while income tax momentum is improving 
and external tax collection continues to contract amid peso 
appreciation.Treasury liquidity also remains relatively tight 
heading into the seasonally demanding year-end period. 
The Treasury’s cash position stood at COP14.9tn as of 
November 6, broadly in line with last year in real terms, 
while net cash balances have declined by an average 
COP18.2tn during November-December over the past three 
years at current prices. Monetization of the recent US$5.2bn 
hard-currency issuance, including the US$2bn Treasury-Ban-
Rep transaction, together with completion of the remaining 
local-currency auctions, should help strengthen liquidity. 
Overall, the fiscal data are beginning to move in an encourag-
ing direction, but the improvement so far rests primarily on 
spending restraint rather than stronger revenues, while the 
sizeable execution backlog means the year-end fiscal picture 
remains less settled than the recent data alone would suggest.
Andeans inflation wrap-up
Regional inflation dynamics diverged sharply in October, 
with Colombia and Chile delivering softer-than-expected 
prints, while inflation surprised markedly to the upside in 
Peru. The composition of the data also differed significantly 
across countries: Colombia continues to show a widening gap 
between easing tradable inflation and sticky services, Chile’s 
underlying inflation momentum remains relatively contained 
despite elevated headline inflation, and Peru is facing a more 
pronounced supply-driven acceleration.
In Colombia, headline CPI rose 0.20%m/m, leaving annual 
inflation broadly unchanged at 6.30%oya. The downside sur-
prise was concentrated in food and core goods, where peso 
appreciation appears to be increasingly filtering through to 
prices. Core goods momentum declined to just 1.11% ar, its 
lowest since 1Q25, but services remain considerably more 
persistent: core services momentum accelerated to 6.69% ar 
and services excluding rents to 7.69%. We maintain our year-
end headline inflation forecast at 6.8%oya, with fiscal adjust-
•
Colombia: High-frequency data point to early signs of 
fiscal restraint
•
October inflation came in below expectations on low-
er-than-anticipated food and goods inflation
•
Chile: October inflation surprised lower, and core 
inflation remains contained
•
The softer data do not take a December hike off the 
table, which remains data-dependent
Colombia: Spending starts to hit the breaks
High-frequency data point to the first signs of spending 
restraint. Our fiscal proxy suggests the primary deficit 
reached roughly 1.9% of GDP through October, compared 
with 2.4% over the same period last year. Importantly, 
through July, before the De la Espriella administration took 
office, the 2026 deficit had been broadly tracking last year’s 
level, suggesting that most of the subsequent improvement 
has occurred since August. Still, spending seasonality 
remains unfavorable in November-December and a signifi-
cant backlog is likely to be executed before year-end. We 
therefore continue to expect the 2026 primary deficit at 
around 3.5% of GDP, broadly in line with 2025. For 2027, 
our baseline assumes that, despite some dilution of the Fiscal 
Adjustment Law in Congress, a meaningful consolidation 
effort will ultimately be delivered, narrowing the primary def-
icit to around 2.6% of GDP.
The most encouraging development has been the marked 
moderation in primary spending. Budget execution excluding 
debt service reached 68.5% through October, still above pre-
pandemic norms, but the pace has slowed substantially since 
the change in administration. Real executed spending 
increased 9.8%oya in January-July, before contracting 
2.6%oya in August-October. Investment has driven most of 
the adjustment, falling 27.2%oya in real terms over the latter 
period, although current spending growth has also decelerated 
(Figure 1). As a share of GDP, primary spending declined at 
an annualized pace of around 0.5%-pt of GDP relative to the 
same period last year, providing early evidence that the new 
administration has begun to put the brakes on spending.There 
are nevertheless reasons to remain cautious about extrapolat-
ing the recent pace of adjustment. Committed but not yet exe-
cuted spending stood at COP49.5tn through October, equiva-
lent to roughly 2.5% of GDP, compared with around 2.2% a 
year earlier. Almost half of this backlog corresponds to cur-
rent spending, which is less flexible and therefore harder to 
postpone, leaving scope for a pickup in execution during the 
final two months of the year or for some spending to be car-
ried into 2027.

Chile delivered a second consecutive downside surprise, with 
headline CPI rising 0.48%m/m and core CPI just 0.02%m/m.

Peru provides the clearest contrast, as weather-related supply 
shocks are pushing headline inflation higher earlier and with 
greater force than we anticipated. The El Niño Costero shock 
lifted annual inflation to 5.25%oya, while sequential headline 
momentum reached 5.6% ar. The underlying picture remains 
considerably more benign, however, suggesting that the 
recent acceleration primarily reflects supply-side pressures 
rather than a broader deterioration in inflation dynamics.

 Ahead of next week’s release we are making some adjust-
ments to the broader inflation profile to reflect recent energy 
prices moves and to allow for greater indirect effects. Oil 
prices have fallen from their peak, which lowers our near-
term inflation profile. We also build in a greater allowance for 
people on fixed rate energy tariffs in January, which acts to 
smooth the impact on the CPI relative to Ofgem’s price cap. 
We assume 60% of people will be on fixed contracts by Janu-
ary, up from around 40% currently. This results in average 
bills rising by 18% in that month versus Ofgem’s anticipated 
24% rise in the cap. This leaves us with a 4.3% CPI peak for 
1Q (down from 4.6%).
We also revise our outlook for food inflation higher to reflect 
the drift upwards in agricultural commodities and align it 
more closely to our model. That model anticipates a peak of 
over 4%oya by next summer, and this change adds 13bps to 
our CPI forecast. We also build in stronger indirect effects for 
next year, worth 0.3%-pts. This is in addition to the 0.2% we 
penciled in a month ago.
In our latest forecast, inflation doesn’t drop below 4% until 
next September and core doesn’t fall below 3% until then 
(previously April). Despite this, our forecast - which is still 
subject to further changes after next week’s data - remains 
lower than the BoE’s and particularly so from the middle of 
2027 (Figure 1). Some of this may be explained by the drop 
in oil prices since the MPR.
