The BoE offers insufficient detail in its forecast with which to 
pinpoint where the exact differences come from. But the tone 
of the MPR suggests the gap may relate to stronger assump-
tions about indirect and second-round effects. We assume 
underlying wage growth next year of 3.5%. There is upside 
risk to this, but it is in line with the BoE’s assumption. We 
think the BoE’s inflation forecast looks to high in 2H27.
Data releases and forecasts
Week of November 16-20
•
GDP continues this year’s outperformance
•
BoE is close to hiking in December, highlighted by 
Lombardelli again this week
•
Rebound in natural gas this week gives BoE little relief
•
We will reassess the timing and magnitude of BoE 
hikes in our forecast after next week’s CPI
•
This week we made adjustments to our CPI profile, 
but this remains below the BoE’s 2027 forecast
Growth continued this year’s outperformance with a strong 
gain of 0.7%q/q in 3Q (3% annualized). Gains were broad-
based across final domestic demand. While this was broadly 
as expected, there was also a stronger than expected gain in 
September GDP of 0.2%m/m. This will provide a tailwind 
worth about 15bps going into 4Q, while the latest October 
PMI also suggests that growth is holding up during the quar-
ter. This week, we hence revised up our forecast for 4Q GDP 
growth from 1.0% to 1.5% annualized, although owing to an 
anticipated energy bill surge in January we also shaved 
0.6%-pts off 1Q growth and now look for a 1% gain.
The MPC last week indicated that it is close to hiking again in 
December, and this point was highlighted again by Lom-
bardelli in a speech this week. She spoke of structural damage 
to the labour market stemming from low churn, which could 
result in higher wage growth than otherwise. At the same 
time, Lombardelli argued to “proceed gradually” if the picture 
remained unclear and that weaker inflation pressures could 
warrant waiting longer.
The drop in energy prices last week suggested the MPC could 
hold off until February before delivering the next hike. But 
prices this week have rebounded, particularly natural gas. 
Taken with the still strong GDP data and the coming inflation 
peak, the case for the BoE to deliver more is growing. We 
look for one additional hike in February. But after next 
week’s October CPI we will reassess both the likely timing of 
the next move and the prospect that the MPC will need to 
deliver more tightening in total over the coming months.
For next week’s CPI we look for an acceleration in headline 
from 3.5% to 3.8%oya with core holding at 2.8%oya. At the 
time of writing, the consensus is lower than this, but we 
expect energy bills in the CPI to rise by more than suggested 
by the Ofgem cap as people who were on fixed rate deals roll 
off onto higher prices (more on this below). While on services 
we (3.8%) are below the BoE (3.9%), the BoE will be looking 
closely at core services. This metric has firmed to a 4.6% 
3m/3m annualized pace in recent months and a gain next 
week of over 0.3%m/m, sa would be troubling to the BoE.

Headline AWE pay growth slowed by two tenths to 3.7%oya 
in the three months to August, which was marginally softer 
than expected. But non-bonus pay was firmer, and private 
sector pay (excluding bonuses) rose for the first time on an 
oya basis since late-2024. We look for a slightly stronger 
monthly gain in the latter of 0.3%m/m in September. This 
would lift the annual rate of growth in private sector regular 
earnings by a tenth to 3.1% in the three months to September. 
However, we anticipate a modest fall in headline whole econ-
omy total wage growth, down to 3.5% from 3.7% in August. 
Elsewhere, we think payrolls likely rose for a third month 
running, which is guided by the recent improvement in our 
nowcast. Our modelling points to a 0.1%-pt fall in the ILO 
unemployment rate to 4.6% in the three month to September. 
This factors in a pickup in the single-month estimate to 4.9% 
following August’s unusually soft rate of 4.2%, but also the 
impact of June’s very high reading of 5.3% falling out of the 
three-month rolling average.

We expect October headline inflation to rise 0.3%-pts next 
week to 3.8%oya (BoE: 3.7%oya). Domestic energy and liq-
uid fuels prices are expected to rise in the month. Ofgem’s 
3.6% increase in the energy price cap took effect in October 
and was a little larger than the 2.0% increase during the same 
period last year. This will push up the annual comparison. 
However, we look for a larger increase in energy bills as 
some people on fixed contracts roll off onto higher prices. 
Meanwhile, the monthly gain in petrol and diesel prices will 
add to the upward pressure.
Evidence of indirect energy effects and second-round effects 
remain very limited to date, and we look for core inflation to 
remain steady at 2.8%oya in October. Beneath the surface, we 
expect core goods and services inflation to both edge higher 
by a tenth to 1.1%oya and 3.8%, respectively, with the latter 
largely reflecting a larger monthly increase in transport ser-
vices prices. We see some upside risk to core inflation, but the 
real focus will be on whether the recent firming in core ser-
vices inflation continues. We assume a 0.3%m/m driven by 
firm gains in catering and other recreational services.
We look for food prices to show another 0.3%m/m gain in 
October, which would result in the annual rate falling to 
1.1%oya. The BoE expects a move sideways at 1.3%oya, 
although this would require a 0.5%m/m, sa gain and hence a 
clear sequential firming not seen to date. A crystallization of 
this would go some way towards validating the BoE’s view 
that food inflation is set to accelerate sharply from here.

Monthly GDP rose 0.2%m/m in September, again exceeding 
expectations. The month’s rise in activity largely came from 
consumer-facing services (+0.6%m/m) and manufacturing 
(+0.7%). Despite a healthy expansion in real estate services, 
key business sectors, such as IT and professional services, 
were notably weaker in September with declines of 
0.6-0.7%m/m. Elsewhere, there was weakness in tech sensi-
tive components, including computer programming and relat-
ed services (-1.9%m/m), ending a run of strong gains in 
recent months and leaving the AI contribution to GDP growth 
over the past year in the +0.1-0.2%-pt region. 
The strong gain in 3Q GDP was driven by a strong expansion 
in domestic final demand, which added 0.8%-pts to non-an-
nualized growth overall. Consumption remained resilient, ris-
ing 0.6%q/q non-annualized in both the private and public 
sectors. This partly reflected a willingness among households 
to dip into savings, but employee compensation also grew 
solidly in real terms. Gross fixed capital investment was up 
1.7%q/q, with public investment growing 13%, although the 
gain in business investment (0.4%) was a little disappointing 
after a period of outperformance earlier this year. Trade vol-
umes rose strongly, with exports up 1.6%q/q and imports ris-
ing 1.8%, but the net trade contribution was slightly negative.

The seasonally-adjusted core momentum slowed to about 4% 
annualized rate in October from almost 6% in September. The 
3m/3m saar rate is at 5% and we expect it to slow further in 
the period ahead (Figure 1).
The two core components that are most relevant to us in 
understanding underlying inflationary dynamics – market-
based goods and services – show a mixed pattern: core goods 
inflation continues to slow despite a succession of shocks 
from the exchange rate weakening, with October momentum 
being below 4% saar, while core services remains sticky at 
8.5% saar last month (Figure 2). The trends in the 3m/3m saar 
are encouraging in the case of both measures, but the elevated 
persistence of core services inflation is hard to reconcile with 
a very large and negative output gap (most likely) and the 
sharp slowing in wage growth (only 5%oya in September 
data, the lowest in the CEE). One way to reconcile such con-
tradictions is through inflation expectations, which are not 
properly anchored given that the NBR has historically 
allowed inflation to stay above target; there could also be var-
ious inefficiencies in the services sector.

Our revised forecast sees headline CPI reaching 7.3%oya at 
end-26 (from 7% previously), but end-27 remains unchanged 
at 4.3%oya. This is partly driven by revised core inflation 
(higher by 0.2%-pts) to reflect more persistent core services,

… but next NBR move likely a cut, not a

Romania’s monetary outlook is shifting gradually toward eas-
ing despite persistent domestic inflation and tightening from 
global central banks. The National Bank of Romania (NBR) 
•

Poland: Growth is still good, but slowing on trend
After a data-rich week, our assessment remains that Roma-
nia’s policy mix is moving towards a more balanced position, 
though the transition is likely to be gradual. Inflation remains 
elevated, particularly in services, while weak consumption 
and slowing wage pressures are gradually creating room for 
monetary easing; we think rate cuts are possible from May 
2027. Political tensions have eased and fiscal consolidation is 
progressing, though budget negotiations, defence spending 
and potential reductions in EU grants remain important risks.
Elsewhere in the region, headline inflation in Hungary 
remains low, which, combined with lower global oil prices, 
reopens the window for the NBH to consider lower rates. We 
think it will cut, but not as soon as next week. Poland’s econ-
omy remains the most robust in the region, but growth is 
slowing on trend. With core CPI momentum having fallen 
substantially of late, we do not expect the NBP to hike this 
year. The odds of a move are higher next year, but our base 
case remains for rates to stay on hold throughout 2027.
Romania: inflation higher near-term
Headline CPI rose to 6.9%oya in October from 6.6%, while 
core inflation eased to 6.0% from 6.1% in September. The 
jump in headline CPI in October was expected. Core goods 
inflation is moderating, but core services remain sticky.

kept its policy rate at 6.50%, but its tone has become more

Romania: Lower political tension, steady 
fiscal improvement
The political situation in Romania is going through a period 
of reduced tensions after the Niculescu government was 
approved in parliament. On the positive side, PSD leaders and 
ministers have spoken in a cautious manner about wage and 
pension increases - the proposed increases are in line with 
inflation (7-7.5%) and likely to be below nominal GDP 
growth. At the same time, the Finance Minister has started the 
process for the 2027 budget. Its approval requires support 
from either PNL or AUR - we think PSD is more likely to get 
support from PNL which has as a main condition a low bud-
get deficit, not more than 5.3% of GDP. We expect talks on 
the 2027 budget to intensify in December and the budget to 
be approved in January or February. 
Fiscal consolidation is progressing, although more gradually 
than planned. The 2026 cash deficit is tracking at around 6% 
of GDP, substantially better than in 2025 and close to the gov-
ernment’s 6.2% target, despite higher defence spending. Fore-
casts now put the 2026 cash and ESA deficits at 6.1% and 
5.8% of GDP respectively, with stronger EU inflows poten-
tially helping towards year-end; cancellation of some grants 
is, however, a risk for the wider cash deficit. Fiscal consolida-
tion should continue in 2027, with deficits declining toward 
5.1-5.2% of GDP, mainly because spending is expected to 
grow more slowly than nominal GDP. Defence spending and 
potential reductions in EU grants remain key fiscal risks. 
Overall, Romania is moving toward a more balanced policy 
mix, but sticky services inflation and still-large fiscal deficits 
mean monetary and fiscal normalization will remain gradual. 
Romania: revising 2026 GDP 
GDP growth jumped strongly in 3Q26, even more than our 
out of consensus forecast. Flash GDP showed growth of 2.1% 
q/q, sa (8.7% annualized) compared to 1% expected by JPM 
(4.1% annualized) and 0.5% by Bloomberg consensus (2% 
annualized). In over-year-ago terms, this is also a strong 
upside surprise: 0.5%oya vs. -0.8% expected on the chain 
linked series (from -1.9%oya in 2Q26). The equivalent based 
on the old method of calculation is +0.1%oya from -0.2% (no 
q/q changes are reported for the old series). 
The strong result was most likely driven by EU-related 
investments under the RRF and, possibly, by a strong agricul-
tural year (this should be visible in a rebound in consumption 
when detailed GDFP is released in early December).  Fixed 
investment is visible in construction output, while the 
rebound in consumption is captured by retail sales (Figure 3).

Hungary: Headline CPI rose in October, but 
remains low
Hungarian CPI increased to 2.0%oya, from 1.6%oya previ-
ously (Figure 4), bang in line with expectations (Consensus 
and J.P. Morgan: 2.0%). Headline inflation therefore remains 
remarkably muted, although the composition continues to 
suggest that such low inflation is unlikely to be sustained. 
Similarly to the rest of the region, food prices have started to 
rebound after very muted dynamics in recent months. Food 
CPI rose 0.2%m/m, pushing the 12-month rate up from 
-5.2%oya to -4.6%. Fuel prices, which through most of the 
year had been more benign than elsewhere in CEE, came in 
on the strong side this time, rising 3.0%m/m. This pushed 
fuel CPI to 11.3%oya, from 6.9% previously, the highest rate 
this year.

Hungary: Window reopens for NBH easing, 
but likely in December only
The combination of low headline CPI and a more favorable 
external backdrop reopens the window for NBH cuts. The 
central bank has maintained a dovish inclination and, in our 
view, only paused its easing cycle because of the challenging 
external backdrop. The recent moderation in oil prices now 
allows the conversation about rate cuts to restart. The NBH 
retains a more benign view of the inflation outlook than we 
do, while its dovish stance is partly validated by the persis-
tence of low headline CPI and subdued core inflation (includ-
ing processed food). We therefore continue to expect the 
NBH to resume rate cuts. However, at next week’s meeting 
the central bank will likely hold and wait for December to 
deliver a 25bp rate cut, accompanied by a new inflation report 
and macro forecast. This will likely be followed by another 
cut in 1Q27, taking the policy rate to 5%. 
Poland: Growth good, but slowing
According to the flash release, the economy expanded at a 
3.2%q/q saar pace in 3Q26 (Figure 6), roughly as expected 
and confirming a gradual slowdown from an average 3.4% in 
1H26 and 4.1% in 2025. In over-year-ago terms, growth 
slowed to 3.5%oya (from 4.1%), the lowest since the first half 
of last year. For now, we lack breakdown details, but we 
know that, on trend, consumption has slowed as real wage 
growth has weakened, while the expansion has rotated some-
what more towards investment, especially as we approach the 
final stages of RRF fund utilization. In 3Q26, construction 
performed very poorly, however, which could signal that vol-
atile investment spending was weaker. Into next year, invest-
ment is likely to slow due to the end of RRF projects, though 
that decline should be cushioned by increased utilization of 
cohesion funds, of which there is still a substantial amount 
available. We expect growth to slow from 3.7% this year to 
3.0% in 2027.

Inflation for the month of October came in fully in line with 
our forecast and consensus. However, core was a tenth above 
our forecast at 0.5%m/m, while we expected a softening to 
0.4% – this comes after 0.6%m/m in September. Core infla-
tion still slowed to 6%oya from 6.1% in September. The sur-
prise in underlying inflation was driven by core services rath-
er than core goods, but even here, once seasonally adjusted, 
the data show softening, just not as much as we had expected. 
A big upside factor expected for October was hikes in elec-
tricity prices as the largest hydro-power generator was 
impacted by the summer drought. Electricity prices did 
increase (2.4% m/m in October, less than the expected 4.8% 
m/m), but this favorable error was offset by a larger increase 
in fuel inflation (2% versus 1.4%), along with hikes in natural 
gas and heating. All these are driving the re-acceleration in 
non-food inflation.

•
Dovish CBRT guidance reinforces our call for a 100bp 
rate cut in December
•
CBRT revised up its end-27 inflation forecast from 
15% to 21%, as expected
•
Hard data confirm softer domestic demand in 3Q26
•
FX reserves fell on foreign outflows in September
This week, the CBRT`s Inflation Report contained no materi-
al surprises on its inflation forecasts. Governor Karahan's 
dovish tone reinforced our view that the rate-cutting cycle 
will continue. In line with our expectations, the CBRT raised 
its end-26 inflation forecast to 29.5%, from 28%, and brought 
its end-27 forecast in line with the Medium-Term Programme 
(MTP) at 21%, from 15%. 
More importantly, the Governor emphasized that MPC deci-
sions are guided by the underlying inflation trend rather than 
headline inflation, which remains affected by supply-side fac-
tors, particularly food and energy prices. Karahan also noted 
that the risks around the inflation outlook, previously seen as 
tilted to the upside, are now more balanced. He attributed this 
shift to slowing domestic demand and benign core inflation 
momentum, despite elevated Brent crude oil prices. His 
remarks on the potential economic impact of recent invest-
ment fund developments also suggest that the CBRT is atten-
tive to downside risks to growth. Following this communica-
tion, we have greater confidence in our forecast for four 
consecutive 100bp rate cuts on 10 December, 21 January, 18 
March and 22 April, bringing the policy rate from 36% to a 
terminal rate of 32%.
The CBRT also eased credit growth caps this week, a day 
before the Inflation Report press briefing. Eight-week loan 
growth limits were raised by 0.5%-pts across all loan catego-
ries, to 3.5% for general-purpose and vehicle loans, 5.5% for 
TL-denominated SME loans and 2.5% for TL-denominated 
non-SME corporate loans. Beyond supporting monetary 
transmission as the easing cycle continues, the move should 
also provide some relief to economic activity, which has been 
weighed down by the investment fund-related volatility. Tak-
en together, the looser credit caps and the Governor's remarks 
suggest that the CBRT is comfortable continuing to ease poli-
cy as domestic demand slows. 
Hard data confirm softer demand in 3Q26
September's industrial production data showed a sharp 
rebound in the headline figure, but underlying industrial 
activity remains weak. Industrial production rose by 2.1% m/
m sa, largely reversing the cumulative contraction of July and 
August. However, most of the increase came from two high-
tech industries rather than from a broad-based recovery. Pro-
duction of other transport equipment, a category that includes

Chain store sales soft in September
Store sales declined 1.1%m/m, sa, in September and rose only 
a modest 1.3%q/q, saar, in 3Q (Figure 3). This confirms the 
message from other consumer-related indicators — the con-
sumer took a pause last quarter. Our consumption nowcaster 
points to stagnation in consumer demand in 3Q. Higher-fre-
quency credit card data suggests that consumer spending 
firmed in October before sliding back lower in November. 
Soft consumption is one reason we don’t expect 3Q GDP to 
be particularly strong next week. Excluding exports produced 
abroad, with think 3Q GDP will be in the 3.0-5.0%q/q, saar, 
range, i.e. at or slightly above potential growth.

Budget deficit up to to 3.3% of GDP in October

Budget deficit increased a tick to 3.3% of GDP in the 12 
months through October from 3.2% in September (Figure 1). 
The story of revenues’ outperformance remained largely 
intact (up 6.5%oya in October), with both income (12.1%) 
and indirect taxes (6.2%) running strong. The main change 
vs. recent months was a substantial increase in government 
spending last month. Non-defense spending jumped 
11.8%oya in October, with broad-based increases across the 
main lines — the holiday schedule may have played a role. 
Defense spending remained elevated, tracking at about 8% of 
GDP despite the absence of substantial military campaigns. 
Although the MinFin has guided for higher expenditures 
going into year-end, we think that barring a jump in defense 
outlays, the deficit should remain within 4% of GDP in 2026.

On a related note, press reports this past week suggest that the 
US military aide to Israel may not be abruptly cut off next 
year, which contradicts President Trump’s comments last 
week. The financing in 2027-28 may continue as planned; 
moreover, the US and Israel are reportedly negotiating a 
5-year Memorandum of Understanding for 2029-24, which 
would keep the US foreign military financing for Israel at 
about current levels ($3.3bn a year), but would not be extend-
ed beyond this period. If confirmed, this would significantly 
reduce the pressure on Israel’s budget in the medium term. As 
a reminder, the current financing package is worth around 
0.5% of Israel’s GDP.

F'cst
Calculated coalition chess 
Coalition negotiations are likely to heat up over the next week 
as the 26 November deadline approaches. While the DA is the 
largest party across the three Gauteng metros, a tense rela-
tionship with ActionSA (ASA) significantly complicates an 
agreement (Table 1). A coalition agreement between the DA 
and ASA is theoretically possible, but unlikely in the key 
metros unless ASA drops its firm demands that DA’s Zille 
withdraws as a mayoral candidate for Johannesburg. A gov-
ernment of local unity (akin to the GNU at the national level) 
that includes the ANC and DA also appears unlikely amid 
push-back from the ANC in favor of a more flexible approach 
for each metro and municipality. At the same time, a coalition 
that excluded the DA in Jo’burg would require participation

We expect the SARB to maintain the policy rate at 7.25% 
next week, following the unanimous hike in September, while 
retaining a tightening bias. A 25bp increase will probably be 
debated at the meeting, but we expect only limited support. In 
our view, inflation projections will be marked up by a margin-
al 0.1%-0.2%-pts to average 4.2% in 2027. In the short term, 
the inflation profile has improved on the back of the recent 
drop in oil prices and the temporary fuel levy cut announced 
in the MTBPS. Moreover, food inflation has continued to sur-
prise on the downside. The combined impact should trim the 
near-term inflation peak to 5.5% (SARB: 5.7%) and delay it 
to February next year (SARB: November ‘26), Figure 1.

In the minutia
The RBA's November meeting minutes are released next 
week and will draw attention for further evidence of the tone 
shift evident in meeting-day communications. The threat of 
further hikes became less explicit in the statement, confirm-

•
New housing loans fall 15.5%q/q, the largest decline 
on record
•
Wage growth forecast to rise 1%q/q in 3Q, as higher 
minimum wage rates take effect
•
Australia’s unemployment rate forecast to fall to 4.6%
Higher interest rates are weighing on the Australian economy, 
with data in the past week continuing to soften in rate-sensi-
tive segments. New housing loans fell 15.5%q/q in the third 
quarter, the largest quarterly decline on record (Figure 1). The 
only comparable falls occurred during the Global Financial 
Crisis (2Q08: -13.3%q/q) and immediately after the onset of 
the COVID-19 pandemic (2Q20: -12.9%q/q). Following 50bp 
of rate hikes in 2H26, we expect home lending to slow fur-
ther, though the large adjustment already seen suggests fur-
ther declines should be less pronounced.

The weakness was concentrated among housing investors, 
where lending values fell a record 32%q/q while owner-occu-
pied lending fell a more modest 5.3%q/q. Compared with pre-
vious RBA hiking cycles, the slowdown in owner-occupier 
lending appears broadly consistent with past episodes. Inves-
tor lending, however, has declined much more sharply than in 
previous hiking cycles (Figure 2), suggesting that higher bor-
rowing costs are not the only factor at work. Tax changes 
affecting investment properties announced in the FY27 Bud-
get have likely exacerbated the decline and are likely to 
remain a structural drag on the segment.
Economic sentiment has also trended lower over the year and 
updates on business and consumer confidence this week 
showed further declines (Figure 3). Consumer confidence fell 
4pts to +80.4 (>100 = expansionary), reaching levels last seen 
during the 2022/23 inflation overshoot, while business confi-
dence edged down to -15 (>0 = expansionary). With the full 
effects of earlier interest rate hikes still to feed through and 
activity likely to remain subdued into next year, we see few 
near-term catalysts for a recovery in economic sentiment.

cash rate target further”. In the Q&A, Bullock stated that it 
was prudent to "take stock" of recent rate hikes and that infla-
tion through 2027 was already "baked in". Words to a similar 
effect were also conveyed at the September meeting, but were 
walked back in inter-meeting communications. With the vote

We will also look for any discussion of the bank's recent 
upward revision to its NAIRU assumption. This adjustment 
has assisted the bank from a "narrative perspective" in "cen-
tralising upside inflation risks" as detailed in a recent Free-
dom of Information request. In downplaying recent increases 
to the unemployment rate, the bank has sought to “reinforce 
the anchoring” of inflation expectations. Importantly, the Sep-
tember minutes reiterated that “even if part of the medium-
term persistence in inflation is driven by factors beyond the 
influence of monetary policy”. Any evolution on this perspec-
tive in the November minutes is therefore likely to have 
meaningful implications for monetary policy next year, when 
we expect inflation to cool towards the target band.

Australia’s labor market has softened over 2026 and while 
wage growth in the second quarter was stable at a headline 
level, there were signs of moderation in underlying wage out-
comes. Private sector wages rose 0.7%q/q, towards the lower 
end of recent quarterly run-rates. This softness was offset by a 
larger gain in public sector wages (+0.9%q/q). Since then, 
slack in the labor market has increased, which should weigh 
on individual wage agreement outcomes over time and put 
further downward pressure on private sector wages. The Fair 
Work Commission's (FWC) decision to lift the minimum 
wage by more than expected (4.75%) will offset some of that 
downward pressure in 3Q, adding about 0.2%-pts to the 
aggregate WPI. If realised, this would see quarterly wage 
growth print at 1%q/q, lifting the annual run rate to 3.4%oya.

retreat should temper cost pressure if sustained. A broader 
turn depends on fiscal support translating into stronger private 
orders, employment and household income.
Imports surge on higher energy purchases
China’s export growth eased in October, declining 1.0%m/m 
sa, following a 6.5% jump in September, leaving annual 
growth at a still-strong 35.2%oya. In the details, ADP exports 
rose further, while integrated circuits, mobile phones, autos 
and low-end consumer-goods declined. Import growth picked 
up further, rising 6.5%m/m sa or 41.6%oya (Fig 2), supported 
by stronger energy and technology imports. Crude oil import 
volumes rose 4.6%m/m sa, while coal and high-tech imports 
also increased. Price effects remained supportive. Global 
commodity prices rallied, while unit prices for integrated cir-
cuits and mobile phones continued to rise, consistent with the 
strong October PPI. As a result, the trade surplus moderated 
in October after a record-setting September. The full-year sur-
plus is on track for around $1.3tr, vs $1.2tr last year.

Looking ahead, the US midterm elections add some uncer-
tainty to China’s trade outlook. Tighter potential restrictions 
on US data-center development could slow AI investment and 
potentially weigh on China’s exports of data-center-related 
equipment, including power equipment, batteries and optical 
components. However, the impact is likely to be more medi-
um-term, as much of the 2027 capex pipeline is already under 
construction or funded. Moreover, China’s direct exposure to 
US AI demand is relatively limited compared with Korea and 
Taiwan, which should contain the broader impact on Chinese 
exports.
FX reserve gains disappoint
China's FX reserves came in softer than expected again in 
October, edging up US$4.9bn to US$3405.2bn, partially 
reflecting the strong imports. With an estimated current 
account surplus at around US$91.4bn and a valuation loss of 
US$6.1bn on a stronger USD during the month, our

The latest trade and inflation data point to resilience, not 
rebalancing. Exports moderated after September’s surge but 
remain supported by manufacturing competitiveness and the 
global tech cycle; stronger imports reflect energy restocking 
and tech inputs, not a household spending revival. Fiscal 
catch-up should support near-term growth, but a demand 
recovery requires support to reach private orders, jobs and 
household incomes.
CPI eases, PPI ticks up
October’s PPI exceeded expectations, while CPI and core 
inflation eased (Figure 1). PPI rose 0.6%m/m sa and 
5.7%oya, driven by producer-goods inflation, while consumer 
goods were broadly stable. Energy dominated: the broader 
energy chain accounted for over 80% of the monthly PPI 
increase, according to NBS. AI-related demand also 
supported electronics and communications equipment prices. 
Meanwhile, CPI eased to 0.7%oya, or up 0.1%m/m sa. Food 
prices fell 0.5%m/m sa, while its annual rate of deflation 
deepened. Transportation and communications provided the 
main lift, while holiday travel supported a smaller rise in 
services prices.

New loan creation records contraction
New loan creation (on a net basis) contracted 100bn yuan, the 
first October contraction on record. Weakness remained 
broad-based across sectors and maturities, dragging loan 
growth another 0.1%pt lower to a fresh low of 4.7%oya. 
RMB loans under TSF contracted by a much larger 590bn 
yuan. Considering the statistical difference with new loan cre-
ation, this suggests that banks’ lending to non-bank financial 
institutions was net positive and increased in October, partly 
reflecting market-stabilization efforts (i.e. stock buyback and 
shareholding-increase relending). TSF growth ticked up by 
0.1%pt to 7.2%oya, with TSF flow at 1151bn yuan, mainly 
on relatively back-loaded government bond issuance this year 
(Figure 4). Corporate bond issuance picked up and shadow 
credit stayed subdued.

The PBOC’s 3Q Monetary Policy Operation Report suggests 
limited room for rate cuts. Lending and mortgage rates are 
already relatively low at around 3%, while further reductions 
would require lower deposit rates to protect banks’ margins. 
With the policy rate at 1.25% following the shift to the over-
night reverse repo rate, we expect no cut before year-end and 
have pushed our forecast for a 10bp cut to 2Q 2027. Recent 
mortgage support, a 25bp cut to the PSL rate and anticipated 
further tightening by major central banks reinforce this 
view. Regarding recent reports of a potential 25% tax on 
CGB and LGB interest, we think the direction of travel 
towards a broader tax base and tighter tax treatment is 
increasingly clear, but implementation is likely to be gradual.
Taiwan: exports hit record high
Taiwan’s trade data surged to fresh records again in October. 
Nominal exports expanded another 8.1%m/m sa, following a 
10.0% gain in September, keeping annual growth elevated at 
58.9%oya (Figure 5). Tech exports rose a further 9.8%m/m 
sa, driven by strength in both electronic components and tele-
com products. Imports were also solid, rising 2.6%m/m sa 
and lifting trend growth to 43.3%3m/3m saar. Capital goods 
imports jumped 13.5%m/m sa, pointing to continued strength 
in domestic AI-related capex. Moreover, net-export contribu-
tion to year-end growth appears sustained, as real exports 
jumped 6.9%m/m sa, while real imports growth was more 
moderate.

Looking ahead, the Blue Wave in the US midterm elections 
has raised concerns over the outlook for data-center invest-
ment, posing a potential downside risk to Taiwan’s AI-driven 
growth. We think the impact on 2027 could be relatively man-
ageable based on our US team’s assessment, as much of the 
spending supports projects that are under construction, per-
mitted and largely funded. The bigger risk lies in 2028 and 
beyond. While a US nationwide data-center moratorium looks 
unlikely, tighter restrictions at the state and local levels could 
delay new projects and flatten the AI capex growth curve. 
Given Taiwan’s outsized exposure to the global AI hardware 
supply chain, a sharper-than-expected moderation in US data-
center investment would likely feed through more visibly to 
Taiwan’s tech sector and overall GDP growth.

October labor data remain constructive
Employment fell 0.2% m/m sa in October, partly reversing 
four months of gains. The labor force also declined, however, 
bringing the seasonally adjusted unemployment rate to a new 
low. Despite the monthly employment pullback, the three-
month trend strengthened further, consistent with a gradual 
recovery in labor-market momentum rather than a renewed 
deterioration.
Job losses were concentrated in consumer-facing private ser-
vices, particularly food and accommodation, while social-
health and public-service employment increased. Manufactur-
ing employment also pulled back after four months of gains, 
and construction employment declined; nevertheless, the 
three-month manufacturing trend accelerated and construc-
tion employment returned to slight growth. These mixed sec-
tor readings suggest that the recovery is continuing, though 
labor-market tightness remains below its level in May 2023 
(Figure 3).

Early November exports point to another record trade 
surplus
•
October employment softened, but the underlying 
trend has improved
Korea’s early-November trade data point to a strong start to 
4Q, with exports extending their long-running uptrend and 
imports also rising sharply, likely reflecting in part the 
strength of tech-related capital-goods demand. Meanwhile, 
October employment dipped after four consecutive monthly 
gains, but the three-month trend continued to improve and the 
jobless rate reached a new low. Overall, the data suggest that 
strong external momentum is continuing alongside a gradual, 
though uneven, recovery in the labor market. October trade-
volume data also confirm that the export strength is translat-
ing into solid real growth, adding support to the 4Q outlook.
Early November exports surge
Customs exports in the first 10 days of November rose sharp-
ly year-over-year; after adjusting for working-day differences, 
the gain remained close to 100% oya. On our seasonal adjust-
ment, exports jumped 16% m/m, sa, extending the monthly 
uptrend to a 13th consecutive month. Although high-frequen-
cy data are volatile, the latest reading reinforces the view that 
the tech cycle continues to support exports and provides a 
strong start to 4Q. The three-month trend in nominal exports 
is also likely to have re-accelerated to a triple-digit annual-
ized pace (Figure 1).

BI preview: A pre-emptive hike
We expect BI to raise its policy rate by 25bp, to 6.00%, after 
a pause in the past four months, as rising expectations of a 
December Fed hike and equity-related concerns add to the 
intensifying FX pressures. Our BI FX Pressure Index (BI-FX-
PI) remains below 1.0 in z-score (Figure 2), suggesting that 
the urgency for policy tightening may not be immediate. 
Nonetheless, we see the hike as a pre-emptive move to limit 
pressure on the rupiah before the Fed’s likely 25bp hike in 
December, with a risk of a more hawkish stance by the Fed.

We acknowledge that this is a close call, as recent economic

BOT’s minutes of the October MPC meeting turned

We reiterate our forecast of three 25bp hikes by the BOT, to 
1.75% in 2027 (Consensus: 1.00%). Our view is supported by 
the BOT’s minutes this week, which guided to a gradual nor-
malization amid the BOT’s concerns about inflation and 
financial stability risks. Next week, we also expect GDP 
growth to have improved materially in 3Q26, alongside the 
upward revision to the GDP series following the manufactur-
ing data revision.
We expect the BI to resume its policy rate hike by 25bp next 
week, to 6.00%, in a pre-emptive move ahead of the likely 
December Fed hike, which has likely contributed to the inten-
sifying FX pressures. USD/IDR rose to its record high this 
week, surpassing its June level of ~18,200. We also expect 
another 25bp hike in December, to 6.25% (Consensus: 6.0%). 
Thailand: BOT turns decisively hawkish
The minutes of the October MPC meeting elaborated on the 
risks of keeping policy rates low for too long, reinforcing the 
case for policy normalization. The BOT indicated that nor-
malization would be gradual and measured, while leaving 
open the possibility of a pre-emptive move. 
The minutes also suggest that monetary policy should operate 
as part of a coordinated policy mix and is not well suited to 
addressing structural concerns. Owing to these arguments, we 
expect at least one hawkish dissenter in December, setting the 
stage for 25bp hikes in March, June and August 2027.
Thailand: 3Q26 GDP preview
We expect real GDP growth to improve to 3.3%oya in 3Q26 
from 1.9% in 2Q26. In sequential terms, this also implies a 
strong rebound to 4.1%q/q saar (1.0% q/q sa) from -0.7% 
(-0.2%). Investment should remain the key driver of growth, 
while consumption should pick up after the weak 2Q26 due to 
the energy shock. Net exports should be a drag on growth, 
reflecting the surge in capital goods, in part reflecting the 
strengthening in investment demand (Figure 1).

Philippines: Large downside GDP surprise
3Q26 GDP decelerated further, to 2.1%oya, significantly 
undershooting expectations (JPM: 3.8%oya; consensus: 
3.1%oya), with drags from household consumption, govern-
ment consumption and net trade offsetting the lift from fixed 
asset investment. In our view, a series of inclement weather 
events played a big role in depressing consumer spending as 
schools/offices closed and work hours contracted. Inflationary 
pressures emanating from the energy shock were also a factor 
via the squeezing of household purchasing power. 
We revised our 2026/27 GDP estimates lower, from 
3.5%/5.3% to 2.7%/5.0%, following the 3Q26 GDP miss, but 
we still pencil in a sequential recovery in 4Q26 on seasonality 
(e.g., remittances, year-end holidays) and a gradual return to 
trend-like growth in 2027 (Figure 3), driven by moderating, 
albeit still-high, inflation and accelerated public infrastructure 
spending.
In terms of monetary policy, we still adhere to the view that 
the BSP will deliver two 25bp hikes in December and Febru-
ary, but remove the risk of outsized 50bp moves. In fact, the 
central bank published an ad hoc statement addressing the 
weak GDP outturn and effectively reiterated its commitment 
to its price stability mandate (see link).

J P M O R G A N
Food prices rise on El Niño concerns
Falling oil prices since the US mid-term elections had raised 
the prospect of a less threatening inflation trajectory for India 
in the coming months. But those dynamics have been more 
than offset by two other forces in recent days: 
•
First, food prices have firmed sharply in recent days as 
concerns about El Niño have intensified. Cereals and 
pulse prices – whose price increases tend to be more 
sticky and long-lived (compared to volatile vegetable 
prices) -- are increasing rapidly. El Niño has already 
induced a sub-par monsoon with implications for the 
Kharif crop. Now concerns are rising about the winter 
crop from low reservoir levels (lowest since 2015) and a 
slow start to sowing (17% below last year).  
•
Second, corporate results have been much stronger than 
expected. This is positive for growth in the July-Septem-
ber quarter (with GDP growth now tracking an impressive 
8%oya last quarter) but also suggests that corporates were 
able to pass on cost pressures with relative ease.
October CPI rose to 6%, underlying pres-
sures worrying 
Both these dynamics were on display in the October CPI 
which printed at 6.1% close to expectations (J.P. Morgan and 
Consensus: 6%) but whose internals were worrying: 
First, food inflation rose from 7.4% oya in September to 8.5% 
in October. Ominously, the more persistent components of the 
food basket—cereals and pulses—recorded sharp increases in 
October, and the daily data from November showed that this 
has carried into November.
Second, underlying inflation continues to firm and broaden. 
Core-core inflation (core excluding petrol/diesel and precious 
metals) increased another 0.45% m/m, sa in October, such 
that its quarterly annualized momentum (3m/3m, saar) accel-
erated to 4.8% in October (vis-à-vis 3.1% in May). On a year-
on-year basis – which is more sluggish – it firmed to 3.8% in 
October from 3.2% in September. Diffusion and trimmed 
mean indices similarly point to a broadening of underlying 
inflation. The weight of items recording inflation above 4% 
increased to 53% in October from 46% in September. Mean-
while, 5% and 10% trimmed-mean measures jumped to 5.3% 
and 5.0% oya in October from 4.5% and 4.2% in September.
•
We raise our inflation forecasts as food prices rise on 
El Niño concerns 
•
Underlying inflation pressures also rise as firms pass 
through higher costs 
•
We mark up our terminal rate forecast to 6.25% (pre-
viously 5.75%) 
•
3Q GDP growth expected to be strong at 8%oya
We raise our inflation forecasts as food prices rise amid con-
cerns about El Niño and as core inflation firms, with corpo-
rates able to pass through higher input costs relatively easily. 
These dynamics were already evident in the October CPI 
report: inflation came in at 6.1%oya breaching the upper end 
of the RBI’s 6% target for the first time in two years. Inflation 
is forecast to remain near or above the RBI’s upper tolerance 
band through June 2027.  This elevated inflation forecast and 
the firming of underlying inflation would invoke urgency into 
the monetary tightening cycle. We raise our terminal rate 
forecast to 6.25% (previously 5.75%). While our baseline 
case is that the RBI moves 25bps in each of the next three 
meetings (December, February and April) there is a non-trivi-
al risk of a 50 bps hike in December if inflationary risks rise 
further between now and then.  We also expect 3Q GDP 
growth to be strong at 8%oya as a result of which FY27 GDP 
growth is projected at 7%. 
3Q growth expected at 8%oya
We expect GDP growth in 3Q (the September quarter) to 
come in at 8.0%oya, broadly in line with 7.8% growth in 2Q, 
as the economy continues to benefit from a cyclical upturn as 
also reflected in very strong corporate results. 3Q corporate 
results were robust with listed firms’ (excluding financial ser-
vices and petroleum products) operating profit growing by 
~25%oya rising from a strong 22% in 2Q. Profits have 
remained strong despite cost pressures, suggesting that firms 
have been able to pass on higher prices. This trend is also evi-
dent in the CPI data, with core inflation firming in recent 
months. Other high-frequency indicators, including credit 
growth and IP, also point to solid demand.
Although GDP growth was strong in 1H of the fiscal year 
(April to September), we expect it to slow to below 7% in the 
2H. The effects of the GST cuts should progressively fade, 
while the impact of El Niño is expected to begin to weigh on 
growth. Tighter monetary conditions and a slower fiscal 
impulse would also impinge on growth.  For FY27 as a whole, 
we expect GDP growth of 7.0%, up from our previous fore-
cast of 6.8%, and compared to 7.8% in FY26.
India

Figure 2: Core-core* CPI
%oya
%3m/3m, saar
Inflation to stay near 6% till June 2027; rais-
ing terminal rate to 6.25%
The sharp increases in food prices in recent days alongside 
risks around the Rabi crop are likely to keep inflation at or 
above 6% until mid-2027. On the back of the 6.1% October 
print, November CPI is tracking at 6.4%. We therefore now 
expect October-December CPI to average 6.3%  above the 
RBI’s forecast of 6%. More importantly, we expect CPI to 
remain close to 6% till June 2027, in contrast to the RBI’s 
forecast which sees headline CPI gradually softening towards 
5.6% by the middle of 2027. That outlook is not without 
uncertainty. Much depends on how El Nino and food prices 
pressures will evolve; to what extent government efforts to 
tame prices (which have already commenced and include 
open market sales and stock limits) will succeed; and how oil 
prices evolve.  
The combination of headline CPI staying at or above the 6% 
upper-band of the RBI’s inflation target all the way till June 
2027 and a clear firming and broadening of underlying infla-
tion is expected to invoke urgency into the monetary tighten-
ing cycle. We raise our terminal rate forecast to 6.25% (previ-
ously 5.75%). While our baseline case is that the RBI moves 
25bps in each of the next three meetings (December, February 
and April) there is a non-trivial risk of a 50 bps hike in 
December if inflationary risks rise further between now and 
then.
